The Royal Borough of Kensington and Chelsea 
KEY DECISION  
Report Title: REVENUE BUDGET, CAPITAL 
PROGRAMME AND COUNCIL TAX 2026/27 
Date: 10 February 2026 
 
Decision Maker  Leadership Team 
Reporting Officer Mike Curtis – Executive Director of Resources 
Key Decision KD1012893 
Access to information Public (part A) 
Wards All  
 
1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
1.1 This report is a key part of the strategic financial planning process for the 
Council. It builds on the reports considered by Leadership Team on 12 
November 2025 and 13 January 2026. 
1.2 It sets out the latest position on the revenue budget for 2026/27 before Full 
Council sets the Council Tax and Budget for 2026/27 at its meeting on 25 
February 2026. 
1.3 This report concerns the General Fund budget and Capital Programme. The 
Housing Revenue Account (HRA) is ring-fenced from the general fund. The 
proposed 2026/27 budget for the HRA and the revised 30-year Business 
Plan are reported separately on this agenda. 
1.4 In July 2025, the Council published its Medium Term Financial Strategy 
which set out an update on the four-year financial position and the 
framework for spending plans in line with Council Plan priorities. At that time 
this showed a budget shortfall of £40.8m in 2026/27 and then an overall 
shortfall of £117.6m by 2029/30.  
1.5 All Local Authorities have a statutory requirement to set a robust and 
balanced budget and since July work has been underway to close the 
budget gap for 2026/27. This report is for Leadership Team to make 
recommendations to Council on 25 February 2026. 
1.6 The robust and balanced budget position for the General Fund in 2026/27 
includes a significant amount to fund inflation, service demand and other 
pressures. This includes £5.0m specifically for temporary accommodation, 

=== PDF PAGE 2 ===
 
 
£1.5m for adults and £1m for children’s social care, £2.2m other demand 
pressures and growth items in other services (a full breakdown is in 
Appendix 4), £9.8m for contract inflation and £3.8m for the 2026/27 pay 
award.  
1.7 The budget also includes the implementation of £21.1m of new savings 
proposals from departments and corporate workstreams. 
1.8 The budget allows for a Council Tax increase of 2.99% for 2026/27 and a 
2% increase in respect of the Adult Social Care Precept. The overall 
increase is below the 5% referendum threshold outlined by the Government 
for 2026/27 in the Local Government Finance Settlement. 
1.9 To help manage financial risks in addition to the above the budget includes a 
£3.7m corporate contingency budget to deal with in-year budget risks and 
unforeseen pressures and a £1.2m contingency budget specifically to 
manage unforeseen pressures within adults and children’s social care. 
1.10 A general fund working balance of £10m will be maintained in line with the 
Council’s agreed policy and a prudent but appropriate level of earmarked 
reserves is being maintained. 
1.11 Over the period 2025/26-2028/29, the Council will invest general fund capital 
expenditure of £186.2m in the borough’s housing needs, schools and early 
years provision; highways, transport, environment and open space; and 
Council buildings and digital infrastructure.  
1.12 In addition, £430.5m will be invested into the Council’s housing stock 
through the Housing Revenue Account. Full details of the Capital 
Programme are set out in Section 17 and for the HRA within the revised 
Business Plan which is reported elsewhere on this agenda.

3. REASON FOR DECISION 
3.1 The Council is legally required to set a robust and balanced budget and 
Council Tax levels for each financial year. 

=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
 
 
4. BACKGROUND 
4.1 The draft revenue budget and four-year capital programme set out in this 
report have been developed within the framework of the financial strategy 
previously agreed by Leadership Team. The Council Plan 2023 to 2027 sets 
out the overarching ambition to Become the Best Council for a Borough that 
is Greener, Safer, Fairer.  
4.2 The Council, like all other local authorities, continues to operate within a 
wider financial climate of risks and uncertainty.  
4.3 The recommendations made in this report: 
• Are in line with the Council’s approach to setting the budget for 
2026/27 as set out in the Medium-Term Financial Position published 
in July 2025, 
• Formalise and confirm the proposals set out in the Council’s budget 
proposals for 2026/27 as reported to Leadership Team on 12 
November 2025 and which were subject to consultation between 13 
November 2025 to 9 January 2026, 
• Are consistent with the Council’s policy on reserves (Section 18 and 
Appendix 2), 
• Takes into account consultation with residents, local businesses, the 
voluntary sector, individual Select Committees, and Overview and 
Scrutiny Committee on the proposed 2026/27 Council and service 
budgets (set out in Appendices 6 and 7), 
• Takes into account the equalities impact assessment as detailed in 
Appendix 14,  
• Takes into account 2026/27 spending and income pressures that 
have been estimated as part of the budget process, and 
• Takes into account final figures on the Council Tax base, the 
Collection Fund and London Wide levies from outside bodies. 
4.4 The final Local Government Finance Settlement for 2026/27 was 
published on 9 February 2026 and the budget being presented is based 
on this.  Changes from the Provisional Settlement included £236k of 
additional Homelessness & Rough Sleeping Grant and the confirmation 
of Public Health Grant allocations for 2026/27.  

=== PDF PAGE 6 ===
 
 
5. DRAFT REVENUE BUDGET 2026/27- EXPENDITURE 
5.1 In November 2025, Leadership Team agreed a series of budget proposals to 
be launched for consultation. The feedback has been considered in 
preparing the draft budget for next year and is summarised in Appendices 6 
and 7. As a result of this feedback, there are no planned changes to the 
budget reductions that were proposed. 
5.2 Of the 433 respondents to the consultation, most were supportive of savings 
proposals that related to reviewing our property portfolio (91%), to simplifying 
and improving processes (76%), to changing staff levels and conditions 
(74%), to changing service models to meet needs earlier or in new ways to 
avoid higher cost later (73%) and to generating additional income (71%).  
5.3 They were least supportive of reviewing the level and range of services 
(54%), raising fees and charges (59%), and using alternative funding 
streams (67%). 
5.4 Responses to the council tax proposals were mixed with 48% supporting the 
proposed increase but 33% opposing. 60% of respondents supported the 
proposed 100% council tax premium for people with second homes whilst 
26% opposed it.  
5.5 Budget reductions of £22.3m in 2026/27 were launched for consultation in 
November 2025 of which £5.8m were proposed from cross-cutting 
workstreams, £0.9m from corporate budgets, £3.5 million from the voluntary 
redundancy scheme and £12.1m were proposals to reduce service budgets 
or increase income within departments (including some savings proposed at 
previous budget rounds).  
5.6 This final savings proposed for 2026/27 are £21.1m. The difference relates 
to £700k of savings being delayed into 2027/28 as a result of the cyber 
incident, and a reduction in the general fund saving from the voluntary 
redundancy scheme as £866k of related payroll savings relate to savings on 
non-general fund revenue budgets such as the HRA. These were slightly 
offset by an increase in the savings proposed from the capital review. More 
detail on these savings can be found in Appendix 5. 
5.7 Some examples of the savings committed to for 2026/27 are: 
• Increasing prevention initiatives in Adult Social Care to increase the 
focus on helping people live more independently. 
• Reducing and redirecting some public health funded initiatives such 
as stopping an oral health care home pilot, roles to support 
employment and reducing the budgets in services such as air quality 
and physical inclusion.  

=== PDF PAGE 7 ===
 
 
• Using reserves and other sources as funding as efficiently as 
possible to ensure reduced General Fund pressure. 
• Acquiring properties to provide our own supported accommodation 
and temporary accommodation to avoid reliance on costly third-party 
providers.  
• Reviewing our packages of care for individuals with disabilities to 
ensure that they are as cost effective as possible.  
• Increase advertising income on Council-owned assets. 
• Increased parking income following national revision of Penalty 
Charge Notice (PCN) levels.  
• Reduced energy consumption by investing in LED street lighting and 
improvements to the lighting in the Town Hall.  
• Focus on moving on residents from high-cost Temporary 
Accommodation (TA) placements and reducing the reliance on 
nightly lets. 
• Renting out new operational space across the Council’s operational 
estate, as well as generating new long-term income across the 
existing commercial property portfolio. 
• Efficiencies across the Council’s facilities management contracts. 
• Tighter staff pay controls such as increased scrutiny around the 
application of honoraria and more robust salary expectations for new 
starters. 
5.8 Setting a reliable and robust budget means estimating spending pressures 
and budgeting for them accordingly. The proposed 2026/27 budget allows 
for £9.7m of additional budget to cover demand and other pressures 
including £5m specifically for temporary accommodation and £2.5m for 
demographic and demand growth in adult (£1.5m) and children’s (£1m) 
social care. A full breakdown is shown in Appendix 4. 
5.9 The current level of CPI inflation is 3.4% (December 2025). This has fallen 
sharply compared to the 11.1% recorded in October 2022 but is still above 
the Government’s target rate of 2%. As part of budget setting, the Council 
has undertaken a detailed review of all contracts and has calculated around 
£9.8m of contract inflation is likely to be required in 2026/27. 
5.10 Inflation for services provided within adult social care is expected to be 
higher than overall CPI rates, with the key drivers for inflationary pressures 
here driven by increases to the National Living Wage (4.1% increase), 

=== PDF PAGE 8 ===
 
 
London Living Wage (6.9%), and rent inflation for care services. £4.2m has 
been included in the budget as a result of these pressures. 
5.11 The remaining £5.6m of contract inflation provision is for other social care 
and non-social care services. This provision will be held centrally for services 
to draw down if/when these costs materialise. 
5.12 The 2026/27 pay award is not yet known. A provision of 3.2% (£3.8m) has 
been built into the budget and will be held centrally until agreement is 
reached. At the time of writing the Council does not know what the pay 
award will be. If it is more than this then departments will be asked to absorb 
this through their existing budgets. Officers are confident this will be 
achievable through natural delays in recruitment and vacancies. 
5.13 Taking account of spending pressures, budget reductions and other 
adjustments described above, in 2026/27, the Council will spend £691m on 
the day to day running of services to deliver the priorities that are set out in 
the Council Plan. This includes Housing Benefit payments of £114.7m 
(which the Council administers on behalf of Government), Dedicated 
Schools Grant of £79.4m (which the Council passports directly to schools) 
and £26.9m of Public Health funding that was recently announced as 
part of the Final Local Government Finance Settlement. Details are 
summarised in Table 1 and set out in full by service in Appendix 3.

7. GOVERNMENT FUNDING 
7.1 The 2026/27 Provisional Local Government Finance Settlement covering 
three years from 2026/27 to 2028/29 was announced on 17 December 2025 
and confirmed on the 9 February 2026.  
7.2 Full details of the Provisional Settlement were given in the Medium-Term 
Financial Strategy (MTFS) and 2026/27 Budget Setting report that was 
presented to Leadership Team on the 13 January 2026. 
7.3 The Settlement covered three years with any decisions beyond that being 
subject to a future national Spending Review. For the purposes of this year’s 
budget setting though, the Council has extrapolated figures for 2029/30 
using the 2028/29 data from Government’s Settlement to remind members of 
the potential for a significant shortfall in that year.

7.6 As can be seen, the Council’s Core Spending Power is the roughly the same 
across the three years of the settlement reflecting Government’s policy of a 
funding floor in 2026/27 followed by cash flat settlements thereafter (the

recently at the Final Settlement stage).  Again, it should be remembered 
that no figures have been provided for 2029/30. In a similar way to grant 
funding, Council Tax has been extrapolated from the 2028/29 working 
assumption of a 0.5% in increase in the base and a continuation of the 5% 
Council Tax referendum limit. 
8. COUNCIL TAX 2026/27 
8.1 Council Tax income is derived from the number of domestic properties in the 
Borough multiplied by the level of Council Tax set for each property 
according to their banding. 
Council Tax Base 
8.2 The Council Tax base is the number of properties in Bands A to H (adjusted 
for discounts, exemptions and premiums) in the borough expressed as an 
equivalent number of Band D units. 
8.3 Two new Council Tax measures have been factored into the Council Tax 
Base calculation in 2026/27: that the Council Tax Reduction Scheme is 
reduced by 10% and a 100% Second Homes Premium is applied in the 
Borough.  
8.4 At its meeting on 13 January 2026, Leadership Team agreed a revised local 
Council Tax Reduction Scheme for Kensington & Chelsea. The changes 
meant that awards under the previous scheme are to be reduced by 10%, 
i.e. claimants would therefore be required to pay at least 10% of their 
Council Tax charge from April 2026. The scheme was subsequently 
approved by full Council on 28 January 2026.  
8.5 The Levelling Up and Regeneration Act 2023 allowed councils to apply 
premiums to Council Tax after a property has been empty for one year. The 
Council has previously implemented this and financial plans assume the 
Council will continue to charge this premium in 2026/27. 

=== PDF PAGE 13 ===
 
 
8.6 This legislation also gave Councils the power to apply a premium of up to 
100% of the Council Tax to owners of second homes in the Borough. The 
Council has already made the resolution confirming this intention (more than 
12 months prior to the financial year in which the changes will come into 
effect). The budget proposals released for consultation by Leadership Team 
following its 12 November 2025 meeting included a 100% Second Homes 
Premium. The Council Tax Base that has been calculated to set Council Tax 
in this report confirms it is being applied from April 2026 at 100%. These two 
decisions ensure residents make a positive contribution to the Council’s 
budget, and, therefore, their services. 
8.7 The Executive Director of Resources has now calculated the Council Tax 
Base under delegated authority on behalf of the Authority as being 106,506 
for the year 2026/27. This is an increase of 7,900 from 98,606 in 2025/26 
based on a collection rate of 96.50%. 
Council Tax Band D Charge 
8.8 Nationally, the referendum threshold detailed in the draft Local Government 
Finance Settlement allows for an increase of up to 2.99%. In addition, there 
will be a further 2% increase, for the element that is referred to as the Adult 
Social Care precept and the additional income raised will contribute towards 
meeting the demands for adult social care services. 
8.9 For referendum purposes, when the Council’s Band D charge is compared to 
last year’s, the increase must be no more than 4.99% (otherwise the Council 
would be required to hold a referendum). 
8.10 Kensington & Chelsea’s council area also has Garden Square levies which 
are treated as special expenses as part of the Council’s tax base and Band 
D Council Tax level for referendum purposes. There is only one Band D levy, 
so, to calculate the Council overall charge, the total Gardens Squares levy is 
spread across the whole of the Council’s tax base and added to the 
Council’s element to arrive at a Band D charge. In effect, if the Garden 
Squares set their levies above 4.99% then the Council’s own Council Tax 
must be set below it to remain within the referendum limit.  
8.11 The 2026/27 Garden Square levies total is £2.472million compared to 
£2.298 million for 2025/26. Actual council tax levels for properties liable for a 
garden square levy are shown in Appendix 10 and the amount residents 
actually pay will be the 4.99% increase plus their separate specific garden 
square levy. 
8.12 The draft budget for the Council is based upon Council Tax being increased 
by 4.99%, the full referendum limit, and this is what was included in the 
Budget consultation. 

=== PDF PAGE 14 ===
 
 
8.13 Although the decision to increase Council Tax is difficult, implementing the 
full increase will enable the Council to generate an additional £5.5m of 
income to help protect the services our residents value and rely on. Despite 
this increase, Council Tax levels will remain in the lowest quartile in London. 
Council Tax Income 
8.14 As said above Council Tax income to be included in the Council’s budget is 
of the Council Tax Base multiplied by the Band D charge. 
8.15 The average Band D charge including Garden Squares will increase from 
£1,101.21 to £1,156.14, an increase of £54.93 (£1,079.08 and £1,132.93 
respectively excluding Garden Squares). 
8.16 Full details are set out in Appendix 10 which sets out the increase for each 
band. 
9. GREATER LONDON AUTHORITY PRECEPT 2026/27 
9.1 In addition to Kensington and Chelsea, the Council Tax Base is also used by 
the Greater London Authority (GLA) to set their budget as well. The GLA 
Band D Council Tax figure for 2026/27 has been published for consultation 
as £510.51, which is a 4.10% increase or £20.13. The final 2026/27 precept 
will be issued after the London Assembly meets on 26 February 2026.

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
 
 
10. BUSINESS RATES 
10.1 Business Rates are set nationally. The value of business premises is 
determined by the Valuation Office and the Government set the multiplier 
which specifies the pence per pound paid in tax. From April 2026 the 
rateable value of all non-domestic properties in England was updated to 
reflect the property market as at 2024.  
10.2 The Chancellor announced during her Budget Statement that for 2026/27 the 
number of Business Rates multipliers would increase from two to five with a 
new retail, hospitality and leisure (RHL) multiplier set 5p below the relevant 
national multipliers for qualifying properties with rateable values below 
£500,000, funded by a high-value multiplier 2.8p above the national standard 
multiplier for properties with rateable values of £500,000 and above.  
10.3 The threshold between the standard and small multipliers (less than £51,000 
Rateable Value (RV)) will not change. The aim is to provide long-term 
stability for the high street, removing the temporary RHL relief scheme.  
10.4 The Non-Domestic Rating Multipliers for 2026/27 will be as follows in the 
pound: 
• Small business RHL multiplier: 38.2p 
• Small business non-domestic rating multiplier: 43.2p 
• Standard RHL multiplier: 43.0p 
• Standard non-domestic rating multiplier: 48.0p 
• High-value non-domestic rating multiplier: 50.8p 
10.5 Business Rates in Kensington and Chelsea are expected to generate 
£376.1m in 2026/27. After deducting Central Government’s and the GLA’s 
share (33% and 37% respectively), the budget assumes the Council will 
receive £112.9m in business rates but retain just £6.7m after a tariff payment 
is made to Government (this is calculated by Government as being how 
much the Council needs to keep for providing services to its residents). 
11. COLLECTION FUND 
11.1 The Collection fund is a separate account which receives the income 
collected from Council Tax and Business Rates payers and then makes 
payments to the Council, GLA and Government. The Local Authorities 
(Funds) (England) Regulations 1992 (as amended) require an annual 
projection of the balance on 31 March each year. This is because precepting 
authorities share surpluses/deficits and need to take account of these when 
setting their budgets.  

=== PDF PAGE 17 ===
 
 
11.2 The projected in-year surplus (Council share) on the collection fund at the 31 
March 2026 is confirmed as being £1.386m. 
12. FEES AND CHARGES 
12.1 Income from fees and charges and rental income in 2026/27 will provide 
around a quarter of the Council’s total funding. The full set of proposed fees 
and charges for 2026/27 were presented to Leadership Team on 12 
November 2025 and then launched for external consultation. 59% of 
respondents supported the proposals put forward to raise fees and charges.  
12.2 Since November, the only change is that leasehold fees and charges, 
previously omitted from the schedule, are now included for completeness. 
Appendix 15 shows the full schedule. 
13. SUMMARY BUDGET CHANGES 
13.1 In previous sections, this report has set out the changes in the different 
elements of the Council’s budget, and these are now summarised in Table 8 
which sets a robust and balanced budget. The full 2026/27 budget and 
Council Tax levels can be seen in Appendix 1.

14. LOOKING FORWARDS – BEYOND 2026/27 
14.1 The Medium-Term Financial Strategy was published in July 2025. The aim of 
the strategy is to:- 
14.2 Plan the Council’s finances over the period 2026/27 to 2029/30, taking 
account of both the local and national context. 
14.3 Provide the financial framework for the Council’s priorities and ensure that 
these priorities drive the financial strategy and the allocation of financial 
resources; and, 
14.4 Manage and mitigate future budget risks by forward planning and holding 
reserves as appropriate. 
14.5 The Council’s medium term financial position is by necessity fluid and 
responds to reflect the changing circumstances faced by the Council, 
updated priorities and ambitions, the latest budget position and a range of 
external factors such as economic conditions and Government legislation. 
The position will likely continue to change as assumptions are updated in 
light of new information ahead of budget setting in the years ahead. 
14.6 Although a robust and balanced budget is being proposed for 2026/27 
subject to approval by Council on 25 February 2026, the financial position 
beyond 2026/27 is extremely challenging due to the announcements in the 
Government’s Fair Funding Review. At present the Council’s overall budget 
planning position indicates a forecast budget gap for 2027/28 of around 
£21.1m. This does not include any assumption of the Council levying 
additional Council Tax above and beyond the usual Council Tax referendum 
limit as allowed by Government in the Settlement.  
14.7 The financial position remains subject to a number of uncertainties (although 
some of these have been removed due to the 3 year Settlement) and a 
number of factors could change the position both in a favourable and 
adverse way. For example, if cost pressures in temporary accommodation 
and other services continue, inflation continues to remain elevated and 
previously committed savings are not achieved to plan this will adversely 
affect the Council’s financial position.  
14.8 The forecast for future years (2027/28 onwards) makes the following key 
assumptions: 
• Transitional protection for the Council’s Core Spending Power falls 
out in its entirety in 2029/30, 
• Average contract inflation of 3% in 2027/28 and 2% each year 
thereafter, 

=== PDF PAGE 19 ===
 
 
• On average, fees and charges to be increased by 2% each year, 
• Pay Awards of 2% per annum, 
• A modest increase in the Council Tax base each year (on average a 
0.5% increase in properties per annum), 
• It is assumed that any reductions in service specific grants can and 
will be contained within service budgets, 
• Spending pressures are recognised and risks have been highlighted 
in Section 18, 
• All savings are achieved to plan, 
• Council Tax and Adult Social Care Precept increases of 3% and 2% 
respectively, 
• No planned use of reserves to balance the budget; and General 
Fund working balance remaining at £10m with no planned use, 
• Empty Homes Premium continue to be levied at the same rate(s) 
• Second Homes Levy continues at 100%.

14.9 Since 2024, the Council has taken a more corporate, centralised approach to 
delivering savings across the Council to ensure greater coordination and 
alignment and established a Savings and Transformation Programme.  
14.10 The programme provides corporate infrastructure to develop and track the 
development and delivery of savings, as well as additional drive to move 
programmes forward. It has support delivery of significant cross-cutting and 
departmental savings over the last few years, including the planned £21.1m 
for the 2026/27 financial year.  
14.11 Executive Directors have led corporate workstreams to transform services 
against the following themes: 
• Better ways of working, 
• Being more businesslike, 
• Changes to service delivery, and 
• Using our money differently. 
14.12 As well as this, “Budget Challenge” sessions took place to scrutinise existing 
spend and contributed towards the savings already committed for 2026/27 
and beyond. At these meetings the relevant Lead Member and Executive 
Director discussed their budget proposals and were challenged by the Lead 
Member for Finance, Digital, and Efficiency, Section 151 Officer, and Chief 
Executive. This helped increase confidence further in savings proposed and 
that the budget the Council is setting will be robust. 
14.13 This approach has enabled the Council to propose £31m of new savings 
over four years. This work will need to continue in order to ensure a 
balanced budget is set for 2027/28 and beyond.  
14.14 During 2026/27, the Council is focused on developing a new targeted 
operating model that will guide how resources are prioritised across the 
Council. This will sit alongside a vision for the sort of council Kensington and 
Chelsea wants to be, which will help us review all services through the same 
lens and unlock longer-term transformation and savings.  
14.15 We are developing a multi-year change programme to support the delivery of 
the target operating model, as well as supporting the work to rebuild and 
improve the Council’s digital infrastructure that has started as part of our 
Cyber recovery programme. 
14.16 A full update of the Council’s Medium-Term Financial Strategy including 
indicative savings from this work will be published in summer 2026 and 
proposals for the 2027/28 budget will need to be developed by Autumn 2026 
to begin consultation with both internal and external stakeholders. 

=== PDF PAGE 21 ===
 
 
15. OPTIONS, ANALYSIS AND PROPOSALS 
15.1 This report represents the outcome of the budget review process for 2026/27 
for recommendation to Full Council on 25 February 2026. However, at this 
point it remains the case that until Full Council sets the budget later this 
month, the 2026/27 budget has not been agreed, meaning that a range of 
alternative options remain open to Members, for both the capital and 
revenue budget. 
15.2 The Leadership Team can amend the proposed revenue budget and Council 
Tax as set out in this report. However, any amendments to the budget must 
ensure that it continues to meet the Council’s statutory requirement for a 
robust and balanced budget. 
15.3 There are a number of areas where Leadership Team could choose to 
consider different scenarios for the budget and recommendations to Full 
Council, such as: 
15.4 Increase/reduce the level of Council Tax for 2026/27. Increasing Council Tax 
above the currently assumed 4.99% (including the Social Care Precept) 
would require the Council to hold a referendum. This is not recommended. 
Setting Council Tax below 4.99% would mean further savings or additional 
income would be required to keep the 2026/27 budget in balance. 
15.5 Deleting or reducing budget savings proposals, subject to considering the 
impact on the overall budget. There would need to be compensating 
offsetting proposals to deliver a balanced budget. 
15.6 Considering and substituting alternative budget savings proposals, whilst 
taking into account the time constraints required to develop these, undertake 
public consultation (where necessary), and meet statutory deadlines for the 
setting of Council Tax. 
15.7 Choose not to apply the 100% premium of Council Tax to owners of second 
homes in the borough from 1 April 2026 and/or change the planned 10% 
reduction in the Council Tax Reduction Scheme. The budget includes £8.4m 
of additional income from these changes from 2026/27 onwards and any 
change in the assumption reducing the level of income generated would 
need to me offset by alternative savings or greater income generation.  
15.8 Changing other assumptions within the financial plans such as assumptions 
about budget pressures, the use of reserves, the level of the centrally held 
contingency and inflation expectations. This could potentially provide a 
benefit to the 2026/27 budget, but this would be a one-off measure and 
could subject the Council to further risk and increase savings needed in 
future years. 

=== PDF PAGE 22 ===
 
 
16. TREASURY MANAGEMENT STRATEGY 
16.1 The areas of Treasury Management and Prudential Borrowing are integral to 
consideration of the Council’s Budget and also need to be considered so that 
Members can assess that capital investment plans are prudent, affordable 
and sustainable. Members’ attention is drawn therefore to the Treasury 
Management Strategy for 2026/27 and details for Prudential Borrowing 
including the Prudential Indicators which are set out in a separate report 
elsewhere on the agenda. 
17. CAPITAL PROGRAMME 
17.1 The Capital Strategy was refreshed and approved by Council in July 2025 
and provides a framework for the allocation of resources to fund capital 
projects. The Council’s ability to prudentially borrow to fund future capital 
schemes is limited by the financial pressures which the Council continues to 
face. Information regarding the revenue implications of prudential borrowing 
is provided in the separate Treasury Management Strategy.  
17.2 The total revenue cost of borrowing costs to deliver the Capital Programme 
in 2026/27 is expected to be approximately £15.1m (£9.0m external interest 
payable and £8.6m minimum revenue provision (MRP), partly offset by 
£2.5m investment income on the Council’s general fund balances). Although 
this is an overall increase from the previous year this is £1.5m lower than 
had been assumed before review and subsequent revenue saving. These 
figures exclude the revenue impact of any borrowing relating to the 
capitalisation direction which will be funded by the Civil Claims Reserve. 
17.3 The Council has been continuing to review its capital programme processes 
to improve its ability to prioritise the programme moving forward, to improve 
the accuracy of its forecasting through critically considering its capacity in 
terms of deliverability, and to therefore ensure the programme is deliverable 
and reflects the priorities in the Council Plan. The revenue impact of the 
Council’s capital investment plans has been calculated predominately on the 
schemes in the capital programme and not the pipeline. 
17.4 The Capital Programme Project Management Office (PMO) has continued its 
work to ensure a tight grip on projects and to control and schedule entry into 
the programme at the right time. This work continues to focus on an 
annualised bidding process and embedding the remit of both the programme 
and pipeline going forward. 
17.5 The Capital Programme contains live projects which are currently being 
delivered and may span over several years. The Capital Pipeline is a 
schedule of future projects that are confirmed to have sufficient justification 
to proceed with project development but are not currently at a point 

=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
 
 
appropriate for delivery. There will be no assumption on any borrowing costs 
until these schemes are moved into the programme. 
17.6 Only projects included within the Capital Pipeline should be considered for 
movement into the Capital Programme to ensure that appropriate 
governance measures are maintained and that projects are reviewed in the 
context of the whole programme. 
17.7 Since the report to Leadership Team on 12 November there has been a 
detailed Lead Member Review of the General Fund Capital Programme 
which has resulted in a number of amendments that have now been included 
in the programme for this report. Table 10 below summarises all movements 
to the General Fund Capital Programme since the original budget was set in 
March 2025:-

17.12 Borrowing is regulated by the ‘Prudential Borrowing Code’. The basic 
principle is that capital expenditure plans must be affordable, prudent and 
sustainable and that prudential indicators are used to support decision 
making. Further details are set out in the Treasury Management Strategy, 
including the Council’s Minimum Revenue Provision Policy for 2026/27. The 
Minimum Revenue Provision is a charge that Councils are required to make 
in their accounts for the repayment of debt.

17.18 The 2015 Spending Review announced the flexibility for local authorities to 
use capital receipts from the sale of non-housing assets to fund revenue set 
up costs of service reform and transformation projects. This has been 
subject to a number of updates the most recent being 26 March 2025. 
Eligible projects are those which are designed to generate ongoing revenue 
savings in the delivery of public services and/or transform service delivery to 
reduce costs or demand for services in future years. In November 2024 the 

=== PDF PAGE 28 ===
 
 
Local Government Finance Policy Statement announced the extension of the 
flexible use of capital receipts to 2030.  
17.19 To date the Council has not made use of this flexibility but potentially may do 
in future, as it could help alleviate some of the pressure on the revenue 
budget. Although any use would have to be considered in the context of 
reductions to the availability of resources to fund the capital programme. 
However, it could be an attractive option for funding implementation costs of 
schemes such as cloud-based IT systems which no longer qualify as capital 
expenditure. Any use of the flexibility will be reported through the quarterly 
finance monitoring reports to Leadership Team. 
18. RISK MANAGEMENT 
18.1 Although the overall position for 2026/27 is a balanced budget there are a 
number of inherent risks for next year and subsequent years. 
Economic Factors 
18.2 The state of the economy can affect Local Authorities in a number of ways. 
Currently the global and UK economy is going through challenging times 
with inflation remaining persistently high, higher interest rates, as well as 
minimal economic growth. 
18.3 The Council’s focus is the impact of economic conditions on the cost of 
services; this includes inflation and interest rates but also the demand for 
services such as social care and homelessness. Services continue to 
consider the potential of factors affecting the economic conditions.  
18.4 Although inflation has been falling in recent years (but most recently it 
increased from 3.2% to 3.4% between November and December 2025) it is 
still above the Bank of England’s target rate of 2%. The Council’s financial 
plans assume inflation on pay and contracts being in line with that 
experienced in 2025/26 but reducing post 2026/27 towards 2%. If it remains 
higher this would be expected to impact on many of the Council’s contracts 
and increase the budget gap in future years.  
18.5 The Bank of England base rate was reduced to 4% at the August Monetary 
Policy Committee (MPC) meeting and again by 0.25% to 3.75% in 
December 2025. The MPC remains sensitive to heightened uncertainty in 
the economic environment, particularly around inflation expectations. Market 
consensus is for a small number of gradual reductions in 2026. 
18.6 Forecasting Publics Work Loan Board (PWLB) rates (which track UK 
Government bond yields) is fraught with difficulty but they are expected to be 
fairly flat (5-6%) over the next three years. Prospects for global growth have 
weakened and this looks set to dampen medium-term UK government yields 
pending clarity on inflation trends. Offsetting this, long-term gilt yields are 

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
 
 
considered to be elevated reflecting uncertainty over US 
economic, monetary and fiscal policy expectations, and potential increases 
in UK and global bond supply.  
18.7 Higher rates increase the cost of borrowing and therefore there can be a 
revenue impact of the Capital Programme. However, it will also increase 
returns on the Council’s cash balances which will be invested through 
applying the Treasury Management Strategy elsewhere on this agenda.  
18.8 The UK economy has again endured a challenging year with the most recent 
figures showing growth currently being relatively flat at 1.5% with an average 
of the same level of growth expected each year 2026 to 2029. 
18.9 The other main concern is the potential impact on the UK economy from 
escalating global instability. The uncertainty around the geopolitical situation 
in the Middle East, on-going war in Ukraine, the impact of the current 
administration in the USA, potential trade wars and the UK’s trading 
relationship with the European Union could increase the volatility of both 
inflation and the ability of the UK economy to grow. 
Local Authority Funding for Years Beyond 2026/27 
18.10 Although now known, providing certainty for the next three years, the Fair 
Funding Review has had a significant detrimental impact on the Council’s 
finances. 
18.11 Transitional funding floor arrangements are in place but even with these the 
Council’s budget in 2027/28 and 2028/29 is still likely to be in deficit by over 
£20m in these two years. It is not known whether any element of protection 
will remain at the end of the 3-year Settlement period (2029/30 onwards). In 
2029/30, for prudence, it has been assumed at this stage that the transitional 
arrangements in the form of a Funding Floor Grant will fall out in its entirety.  
18.12 The new funding formula now in place uses “notional” rather than actual 
Council Tax levels meaning councils that do not increase Council Tax by as 
much as the government expects are penalised by how the formula works. 
Given its low levels of Council Tax, Kensington and Chelsea has been given 
special dispensation to increase Council Tax higher than the current 
referendum limit in 2027/28 and 2028/29 (£150 each year at a Band D 
equivalent has been assumed by Government). 
18.13 The MTFS does not currently assume these exceptional increases in its 
budget as this will be a decision for councillors in future who will have to 
weigh up the service needs of residents versus the impact of big increases 
of Council Tax on them. 

=== PDF PAGE 30 ===
 
 
Temporary Accommodation Pressures 
18.14 The Council currently has just under 2,000 households in temporary 
accommodation. The available social housing stock in the borough is over 
18,000 units, of which 7,000 are council owned.  
18.15 Although the number of households in temporary accommodation has been 
relatively stable over the last few years, expenditure remains high largely 
due to the rising costs of landlord rents caused by limited supply (high 
mortgage rates and taxation changes could tempt landlords to leave the 
market) and competition from other local authorities and more lucrative 
holiday rental alternatives. Other comparatively small contributing factors 
include the void period between moves and costly storage and removal 
costs.  
18.16 The service has focussed on prevention measures to reduce the pressure on 
Temporary Accommodation through the continued use of incentive 
payments to move households into cheaper private rented sector 
accommodation. 
18.17 Growth of £2.5m was included in the 2025/26 budget. Further growth of £5m 
has been built into the Medium-Term Financial Strategy for next year in 
recognition of the cost pressures being experienced and reported as part of 
2025/26’s in-year revenue monitoring processes.  
Demographic and Demand Pressures 
18.18 These pressures particularly impact supporting vulnerable children and 
adults. They have been reviewed and forecast and the budget includes 
£2.5m for them (£1.5m in Adult Social Care and £1m in Children’s Services). 
The estimates take account of historical trends, current intelligence, future 
forecasts, and external advice but there is a clear risk that circumstances will 
change and cost implications exceed expectations resulting in a budget 
shortfall. The budget therefore also includes contingency to help guard 
against these risks. 
Delivery of Savings 
18.19 The Council is in the position to set a balanced budget for 2026/27 in part as 
a result of £21.1m of planned budget reductions. Due diligence has been 
carried out so the Council is confident these can be achieved but will 
continually monitor the delivery of these through the year as part of the 
quarterly financial management reports to Overview and Scrutiny Committee 
and Leadership Team to ensure that any emerging risks can be identified 
early and action taken to mitigate them. 

=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
 
 
Dedicated Schools Grant (DSG) Deficit 
18.20 The financial position of overall school funding is challenging. The DSG had 
a deficit carry forward from 2024/25 of £3.287m. This is expected to increase 
to £3.532m at the end of March 2026. The trend in increasing pupils with 
high needs and associated costs is likely to continue putting further pressure 
on high needs budgets. 
18.21 It should be noted that DSG deficits are currently ringfenced from Local 
Authorities wider finances meaning that they do not have to cover any DSG 
deficit from their general fund resources, known as the “statutory override”.  
18.22 This ringfence has recently been extended to March 2028 (from March 
2026). Government, in its November 2025 Policy Statement, promised to set 
out further details on its plans to support local authorities with their historic 
deficits in the Final Settlement.  
18.23 The Final Local Government Finance Settlement was published on 9 
February 2026, and a new High Needs Stability Grant was announced 
that will cover 90% of the Dedicated Schools Grant (DSG) deficit as at 
31 March 2026. It is expected that this will be circa £3m for Kensington 
and Chelsea. The grant will be paid in Autumn 2026 and is subject to 
verification of eligible costs and each local authority submitting and 
securing the Department for Education’s approval of a local SEND 
reform plan. 
18.24 It is not known at this point, what, if any, support will be provided after this 
year so any further overspends on High Needs DSG could potentially 
detrimentally impact the Council’s revenue budget or reserves position in 
future years. 
18.25 The Council’s last DSG Deficit Management Plan was produced in 
partnership with schools and agreed with the Schools Forum in January 
2024. An update of the deficit management plan is on hold until the 
publication of the government’s Special Needs White paper and the funding 
implications are understood.  
Accounting and valuations of Pooled Investment Funds 
18.26 From April 2018, local authorities were required to comply with the new 
standard IFRS 9. This would have meant that the gains and losses arising 
from the changes in the fair value of some categories of investment would 
consequently impact the general fund. The temporary statutory override that 
was brought in meant councils could transfer any gains or losses to an 
unusable reserve rather than charge Council Taxpayers through the revenue 
budget.  

=== PDF PAGE 32 ===
 
 
18.27 This override, which was due to expire 31 March 2025, has been extended 
to 31 March 2029.  
18.28 The Council has one investment as part of the treasury management 
strategy which could be impacted by this. If the override does end in 2029 
the Council would need to make up the loss funded from the contingency 
budget or a drawdown from reserves during 2029/30. The cost (or gain) for 
this in that year is dependent on market forces between now and then but 
the cost in the Council’s latest Statement of Accounts as at 31 March 2025 
was £1.5m. 
Cyber Incident November 2025  
18.29 Although the outcome of the government’s funding proposals has had 
significant consequences on 2026/27 budget setting, other factors (which 
include the cyber incident) are also expected to affect the Council’s finances 
in a material way.  
18.30 The cyber incident which occurred on the 24 November 2025 has affected 
the whole of the Council’s operations to varying degrees, but one particular 
piece of work has been severely impacted. The Residents Connect 
Programme was brought in to deliver savings to the Council by developing 
systems and processes such as document recognition and in parking. 
Systems access disruption and stakeholder availability has led to both the 
backlog of cases increasing at the same time as staff having to be re-
deployed to deal with the cyber incident itself. It is estimated that around 
£700,000 of the saving anticipated will now not be achievable until 2027/28 
18.31 The collection of Council Tax and Business Rates may also be impacted by 
the cyber incident depending on how quickly systems are up and running 
again. It is not possible to assess this at the present time. 
18.32 Work to recover from the cyber incident is on-going and the full effects of it 
will not be known for some time. However, it will be made up of a 
combination of additional support to recover from the incident and then 
additional investment in new systems moving forward. 
18.33 The Council’s most recent reserves forecast has available around £22m of 
balances in our Budget Stabilisation, Budget Carry Forward, and 
Contingency reserves, some of which could be used to fund one-off costs 
relating to recovery and modernisation following the cyber incident. 
Reserves 
18.34 The nature of risks and uncertainties means it is therefore important that the 
Council takes a prudent approach to assumptions in both next year’s budget 
and medium-term financial plans to hold sufficient levels of contingency 
funding within the budget for dealing with unforeseen circumstances in the 

=== PDF PAGE 33 ===
 
 
short term. Levels of reserves provide a safety net in the event of more 
significant negative impacts on Council funding in the medium term. 
18.35 Section 25 of the Local Government Act 2003 requires the Chief Financial 
Officer to report the robustness of estimates and the adequacy of reserves 
when determining their budget and level of Council Tax. This report therefore 
includes the advice of the Executive Director of Resources as Section 151 
Officer as set out in “Financial and Resource Implications” section. 
18.36 Under the 2003 Act above, the Section 151 Officer has to be satisfied that 
the level of the General Fund working balance is adequate. It is proposed 
that the General Fund balance be maintained at its target level of £10 million 
for 2026/27, which is the same level as 2025/26. 
18.37 The Executive Director of Resources has also reviewed all earmarked 
reserves (monies set aside for a specific purpose). Details of the latest 
estimated position and the forecast for the next three years is set out in 
Appendix 2. 
18.38 The Executive Director of Resources following consultation with the Lead 
Member for Finance, Digital, and Efficiency, can approve all contributions to, 
and appropriations from, reserves up to £250,000. Any such approvals will 
be included in quarterly monitoring reports to Leadership Team for noting. 
Amounts in excess of £250,000 must be approved by Leadership Team, 
subject to any limitations set by the Council in the approved budget 
framework. Any such approvals will be included in quarterly monitoring 
reports to Leadership Team for approval. 
19. CONTINGENCY AND CENTRALLY HELD BUDGETS 
Contingency 
19.1 Historically the Council has maintained a central contingency budget for any 
unforeseen one-off budget pressures, and this has been available to 
manage other risks should they materialise, such as slippage in delivery of 
savings, unexpected events or additional revenue costs of capital schemes 
and new burdens. In 2026/27 the Council has set aside £1.2m specifically for 
any unforeseen pressures within social care and £3.7m as a general 
corporate contingency budget. 
19.2 Although the £3.7m corporate contingency budget can ultimately be used for 
any unforeseen pressures that may materialise during 2026/27, Table 17 
below shows a breakdown of how the Council has broken down the estimate 
of it in total. The social care contingency will only be used to fund pressures 
in Adults and Children’s social care.

19.3 Contract inflation remains a key area of concern given the current economic

will be needed to manage these pressures in 2026/27, of which £4.2m

20. LONDON-WIDE PAYMENTS 
20.1 The Council contributes to a number of London-wide services and some of 
these contributions for 2026/27 have now been confirmed. As can be seen in 
Table 19, the total budget needed for London-wide levies is expected to be 
around £13.4m. 
20.2 The most significant levy relates to concessionary fares and has been 
confirmed at £8.9m for 2026/27. The levy is based on journey numbers per 
London borough and is an increase of £776k compared to 2025/26. This 
increase for 2026/27 is a result of journey numbers continuing to return to 
pre-COVID numbers. This is £552k lower than had been assumed in the 
Council’s original financial plans and this reduction has gone towards setting 
a balanced budget for 2026/27.  
20.3 The Garden Squares and the London Boroughs Grant Scheme have 
also confirmed their levies. The figures for the other bodies in the table 
though are based on estimates. As variations to the estimated numbers 
are not expected to be material, any levies being agreed above them 
can be comfortably managed in 2026/27 through the flexibilities 
outlined by the Section 151 Officer in Section 24 of the report.

21. SCHOOLS BUDGET 
21.1 The total budget delegated to schools in 2026/27 is £92.8m (after the agreed 
transfer to the high needs block) which is an average 1.9% per pupil 

=== PDF PAGE 36 ===
 
 
increase. Every school is protected at 0% per pupil reduction compared to 
2025/26 funding. Overall, there is a reduction of 49 pupils made up of 103 
fewer pupils in primary schools and 54 more pupils in secondary 
schools. Schools will be supported in planning how to develop budget plans 
to deal with any reductions in pupil numbers. 
21.2 The financial position of overall school funding is challenging. The trend in 
increasing pupils with high needs and costs is especially acute and 
continues to put further pressure on high needs budgets. The Dedicated 
Schools Grant deficit balance is covered in the risk section of the report, and 
a deficit management plan is in place to manage this. 
22. CONSULTATION AND COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT 
22.1 This year’s approach to the budget consultation aimed to ensure that 
residents, businesses and voluntary and community organisations had an 
opportunity to review and comment on the budget proposals. This is 
particularly important given the scale of financial challenge over the next few 
years so that all stakeholders could help shape the organisational 
implications of this.  
22.2 The consultation was promoted via the Council’s website and electronic 
noticeboards across the borough as well as through various channels of 
direct engagement such as the resident e-newsletter, business e-newsletter, 
VCS e-newsletter and statutory partners. Social media was also employed.  
22.3 Full details were published on the Council’s website which provided a link to 
the main on-line survey which ran for a period of 8 weeks from 13 November 
2025 to 9 January 2026. A dedicated e-mail address 
(budget@RBKC.gov.uk) was set up for people to contact the Council if they 
wanted to ask questions or request any materials. Hard copies of the 
questionnaire and supporting information was also sent out on request.  
22.4 Scrutiny Select Committees and the Overview and Scrutiny Committee 
formally scrutinised the budget proposals that related to their service areas 
at meetings in November and December. 
22.5 This report was written with input from Senior Management but there was no 
requirement to consult more widely beyond the consultation already carried 
out as it mainly concerns assumptions and reporting on recent Government 
announcements.  
22.6 As a result of the consultation steps above the Council received 433 
responses to the budget proposals for 2026/27 and these are included in a 
separate report as Appendix 6 and members are recommended to consider 
the findings when making any final decisions.  

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
 
 
23. LEGAL IMPLICATIONS 
23.1 Section 25 of the Local Government Act 2003 requires the Council’s Chief 
Financial Officer, to report on the robustness of the estimates made for the 
purpose of calculating Council Tax and the adequacy of council reserves. 
23.2 The Council is required to set a balanced budget and the Local Government 
Finance Act 1992 requires the Council to make estimates of gross revenue 
expenditure and anticipated income, leading to a calculation of a budget 
requirement and the setting of an overall budget and Council Tax. The 
amount of the budget requirement must be sufficient to meet the Council’s 
legal and financial obligations, ensure the proper discharge of its statutory 
duties, and lead to a balanced budget. The budget as proposed is balanced.  
23.3 The Council should be satisfied that the proposals put forward are a 
reasonably prudent use of resources in both the short and long term, and 
that the interests of both Council Taxpayers and ratepayers on the one hand 
and the users of Council services on the other are both taken account of. 
23.4 Under powers contained in the Localism Act 2011, the government can 
require compulsory referenda on Council Tax increases above limits it sets. 
For 2026/27, the provisional limit is 2.99%. The proposal is within the 
threshold, so, if it is approved, the Council will not be required to hold a 
referendum. The government has also provisionally announced an additional 
threshold of 2% for authorities with adult social care responsibilities.  
23.5 Section 106 of the Local Government Finance Act 1992, applies to all 
Members where: 
• they are present at a meeting of the Council, the Cabinet 
(Leadership Team) or a Committee and at the time of the meeting an 
amount of Council Tax is payable by them and has remained unpaid 
for at least two months, and, 
• any budget or Council Tax calculation, or recommendation or 
decision which might affect the making of any such calculation, is the 
subject of consideration at the meeting. 
23.6 If the above criteria apply, any Member shall, at the meeting and as soon as 
practicable after its commencement, disclose the fact that Section 106 
applies to them and shall not vote on any question concerning the matter. 
Members are however still able to speak at the meeting. Failure to comply 
with these requirements constitutes a criminal offence, unless any such 
members can prove they did not know that Section 106 applied to them at 
the time of the meeting or that the matter in question was the subject of 
consideration at the meeting. 

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
 
 
24. FINANCIAL, PROPERTY AND ANY OTHER RESOURCES 
IMPLICATIONS 
24.1 These are addressed throughout the report. In addition, in setting the annual 
budget, Section 25 of the Local Government Finance Act 2003 requires the 
Executive Director of Resources (Section 151 Officer) to report to Members 
on the robustness of budget estimates and the adequacy of proposed 
financial reserves. The Executive Director of Resources comments that: 
“I am satisfied that the budget calculations are robust and that the 
2026/27 budget takes account of all known liabilities and financial risks, 
but in particular it assumes that the Council will not rely on withdrawals 
from reserves to meet recurrent revenue commitments.  
For any unforeseen one-off pressures that materialise during 2026/27 a 
£3.7m corporate contingency budget has been set aside which will be 
able to fund these. Furthermore, there is £5.0m specifically for 
temporary accommodation, £2.5m for social care, £2.2m for demand 
pressures and growth items in other services, £4.2m for social care 
related inflation pressures, £5.6m for contract inflation in other services 
and £3.8m for pay inflation being held centrally that can be utilised.  
Current financial plans assume £30m of savings proposals including 
£21m in the 2026/27 budget alone. This level of savings is challenging 
although achievable and it is essential that these are delivered in full in 
2026/27 for us to deliver on this balanced budget. 
The financial position of overall school funding is challenging with the 
Council’s Dedicated Schools Grant (DSG) forecasting to have a deficit 
of £3.532m at the end of March 2026. Currently DSG deficits are 
ringfenced from Local Authorities wider finances and the government 
has confirmed this will remain the case until March 2028 (along with a 
new High Needs Stability Grant being provided). However, if this 
changes, this could detrimentally impact the Council’s 2028/29’s 
revenue budget or reserves position. 
Section 18 of the report identifies the risks that potentially may impact 
the Council’s budget. The effects for some of them are known (or at 
least can be estimated with some degree of accuracy and provided for) 
but some are less certain such as the final cost of the recent Cyber 
Incident. 
There are some significant costs arising from the cyber attack which 
affected the Council on 24th November 2025. There will be further 
costs in 2026/27, including from the cyber recovery programme. These 
costs have to be fully quantified, but it is planned that they will be met 
from Reserves. 

=== PDF PAGE 39 ===
 
 
I consider that current levels of unallocated reserves remain adequate. 
However, there are a number of demands on them including recovery 
from the cyber-attack on the Council and potentially also continuing 
costs arising from the Grenfell tragedy.  In setting the level of general 
reserves and balances, account has been taken of the key financial 
assumptions underpinning the budget, the level of earmarked reserves 
and provisions, and the risks facing the Council over the medium-term. 
Financial Plans assume that over the planning period the Council is 
forecast to maintain a target of general balances of £10m. Maintaining 
general balances at this level is considered a minimum position. 
Government’s Fair Funding Review is now complete and factored into 
the 4-year forecast.  This has a total impact of £108.5 reduction in 
external funding from government grants and retained business rates. 
The reduction is confirmed as £17.9m in 2026/27 and £61.3m over the 
three year Settlement period. Although I am only required to comment 
on the robustness of calculations and adequacy of reserves in 2026/27, 
large deficits are still currently forecast in 2027/28 and 2028/29 and will 
need to be brought under control in future budget rounds.  Even though 
2029/30’s Fair Funding allocations are not yet known, transitional 
protection arrangements are due to fall out leaving the Council 
potentially with a much larger budget gap in that year.  The Council and 
Government will need to work together to mitigate the impact of this on 
residents’ services as these constitute extremely significant and 
challenging reductions in fundings.” 
25. HUMAN RESOURCES IMPLICATIONS 
25.1 Several of the budget proposals including the voluntary redundancy scheme 
will have an impact on staff. Where they have there has been the 
appropriate consultation in line with the Council’s HR policies. 
26. EQUALITIES IMPACT ASSESSMENT AND IMPLICATIONS 
26.1 The current cumulative impact of the proposed budget across all protected 
characteristics is assessed are as neutral overall. When jointly assessed 
against the individual protected characteristics, the proposals will have a 
neutral impact on all protected characteristics except for socioeconomic 
status and pregnancy and maternity which will be negatively impacted 
overall. 
26.2 Of the 112 proposals the equalities impacts have been assessed as follows. 
The table in Appendix 6 provides more detail on each proposal’s findings. 
• 12 have been assessed by the service area as having a negative 
impact on our residents, services and staff.  
• 19 as having a positive impact.  

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
 
 
• 13 as a neutral impact.  
• 46 have no equalities impacts. 
• 22 have stated the equalities impacts are currently unknown. 
26.3 This year the Council has had to make some difficult decisions on savings 
and therefore compared with previous years there are several proposals 
which will have a negative impact, primarily on socioeconomic status, age, 
disability and race. They will require close monitoring and mitigation as they 
include impacts on some of our most vulnerable residents living in 
deprivation. 
26.4 The following proposals will have a negative impact or where the impact is 
currently unknown has the potential to have a negative impact: 
26.5 Council Tax Reduction Scheme (CTRS) - The review of the CTRS could 
reduce financial support for low-income households, disproportionately 
affecting residents living in deprivation. 
26.6 Introducing a self-funder administration fee for those receiving support from 
adult social care who use the Council’s brokerage service may negatively 
impact residents with limited financial resources, particularly older adults and 
those with disabilities who already face high-care costs. 
26.7 Reductions in digital inclusion initiatives risk increasing digital exclusion 
among residents who cannot afford devices or have sufficient internet 
access. This will disproportionately affect older people, residents with 
disabilities and those from lower socio-economic backgrounds. 
26.8 Reduction in activities for young people. Although specific services have not 
yet been identified, reductions in youth provision are likely to impact young 
people from socio-economically disadvantaged backgrounds, who depend 
on local services for support and engagement. 
26.9 Community Partnerships (Grants) – this proposal is currently consulting with 
partners to understand its full impact. It has been assessed as having a 
negative impact on residents due to reductions in services where 
alternatives cannot be found. 
26.10 Community Partnerships (Rent Subsidy) – this proposal is also currently 
consulting with partners and again has the potential to negatively impact on 
residents where alternative funding cannot be found.

Appendix 1 – Revenue Budget 2026/27 and Medium-Term Financial Position 
 
This Appendix sets out a summary of the Council wide revenue budget for 202 6/27 in the table 
that follows and also the forecast finance position for the following three years.  
 
The Council aligns its financial strategy with its ambitions through a rolling four-year cycle of 
business planning. The current Medium Term Financial Strategy was published in July 2025 and 
can be found under the Leadership Team meeting agenda papers that month.  
 
The aim of the financial strategy is to:- 
• Plan the Council’s finances over the next four years, taking account of both the local and 
national context; 
• Provide the financial framework for the delivery of the Council’s priorities and ensure that 
these priorities drive the financial strategy – allocating our financial resources whilst also 
responding to external factors (e.g. the Covid pandemic) both now and over the next few 
years; and 
• Manage and mitigate future budget risks by forward planning and holding reserves as 
appropriate; 
 
The MTFS is subject to continuous review and refinement in the light of latest information as it 
emerges. The budget position for the next four years, showing the latest resource projections and 
estimates of expenditure, is set out in the following table.  The next update to the Medium -Term 
Financial Strategy will be in summer 2026.

Appendix 2 - Reserves Policy 2026/27 
Reserves are an important part of the Council’s financial strategy and are held to 
create long-term budgetary stability. They enable the Council to manage change 
without an undue impact on Council Taxpayers and are a key element of its strong 
financial standing and resilience. The Council’s key sources of funding face an 
uncertain future, so a limited number of un-earmarked and earmarked reserves and 
a working balance are held to mitigate future financial risks. 
Reserves are formally reviewed and presented to Leadership Team for noting and 
approval as part of the quarterly financial reporting process. This involves scrutiny of 
balances to determine whether the original purpose for creating the reserve still 
stands, and whether there is scope to release the reserve balance in part or in full. 
The terms of reference for usable reserves set out the conditions for use of existing 
reserves and new applications for funding, and this acts as a source of reference to 
support clear and transparent decision making.  
The table below shows the 2024/25 closing /2025/26 opening reserve balances and 
the four-year forecast position at Quarter 2. The forecast will be further updated 
following the reporting of the Quarter 3 budget monitoring position to Leadership 
Team in March 2026. Further information on each reserve is also detailed below. 
Table 1 2025/26 Opening Reserve Balances and 4-Year Forecast

Budget Stabilisation 
The Budget Stabilisation Reserve is used to manage the impact of uncertainties, 
such as changes arising from government funding. Estimates of known requirements 
will be set out in the annual Council Tax and Budget Setting Report, or the Quarterly 
Budget Monitoring Reports, where applicable. 
Budget Carry Forward 
The Budget Carry Forward Reserve includes balances set aside in previous years 
for which there may be planned use in future years. The reserve also provides the 
opportunity for resources to be mobilised swiftly to enable the Council to respond 
efficiently in the face of circumstances unforeseen at the time of budget setting. In 
the past, it has been used interchangeably with the Special Projects Reserve with a 
focus on meeting one-off, unbudgeted costs in the short-term. Funds have also been 

=== PDF PAGE 46 ===
committed to supporting Covid-19 recovery and, more recently, the Council’s 
response to the cost-of-living crisis. 
Covid-19 Recovery/Cost of Living 
The Council has previously committed £8.0m to providing support with Covid-19 
recovery, and for managing the response to the cost-of-living crisis and its impact on 
residents and local communities. Funds have been contributed equally from the 
Budget Carry Forward Reserve and Public Health Reserve, and the review process 
is overseen by the Cost-of-Living Financial Resilience Group.  
The Covid-19 Recovery/Cost of Living Reserve is tabled separately, though it is not 
treated as a separate reserve in the Council’s accounts. 
Council Plan Implementation 
This reserve was newly established in 2023/24 with an opening balance of £1.0m. 
Funds will be used to support delivery of the Council Plan and our transformation 
ambition to become the best Council for residents in a borough that is greener, fairer, 
and safer. 
Council Plan Implementation – Ward Improvements 
This reserve was newly established in 2023/24 with an opening balance of £1.0m to 
support delivery of the Council’s cross-cutting ambitions and ward-based 
improvements. It will be used to kick start projects and address one-off and time-
limited needs. Funding will also be one-off and time-limited in nature and may be 
spent on both revenue and capital schemes. 
Special Projects 
The Special Projects Reserve was established with a focus on meeting one-off 
funding requirements. Funds have been used to support service redesign and 
transformation, revenue costs associated with the delivery of capital schemes, and 
pump-priming for invest-to-save projects. With significant budget reductions still to be 
identified, further use will be required in the short-term to meet one-off costs and 
provide support with the identification and delivery of new savings. 
Waste Initiatives 
This reserve was created during 2025/26 funded by the Extended Producer 
Responsibility Grant. The grant is paid for by a charge levied by Government on 
packaging suppliers to help offset the cost to councils of subsequently recycling or 
disposing of the waste generated. Given its unpredictability, the grant was not 
budgeted in the Council’s financial plans – it was set aside in a reserve instead. 
Although no firm plans for its use have currently been agreed, it is available to either 
implement new initiatives in waste collection and disposal, or to protect the Council’s 
waste budget from higher levels of disposed material. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 47 ===
Contingency 
The Council has historically maintained a central contingency budget to manage the 
impact of unforeseen, one-off budget pressures, unexpected events, and risks such 
as slippage in the delivery of savings. At the end of 2022/23, the contingency budget 
reported an underspend, and this surplus was used to establish the Contingency 
Reserve. 
Some use of reserve is planned in 2025/26 to manage pressures that would 
ordinarily be funded from the £3.7m central contingency budget. Financial plans from 
2026/27 assume this budget will be required again, but the reserve will continue. 
Car Parking 
The Car Parking Reserve holds the unapplied surplus generated by the Parking 
Service. There may be transfers to or from the reserve each year depending on the 
amount of eligible spend that is funded from the Parking surplus. The reserve is used 
to fund capital schemes and one-off projects that meet the criteria for spend, such as 
highways maintenance and local environmental improvements. 
Civil Claims Settlement 
The Civil Claims Settlement is being used to fund the revenue implications of the 
Council’s claim for exceptional financial support to help cover the cost of resolving all 
claims that have been lodged since the Grenfell tragedy. It is being funded from 
savings generated by the temporary reduction to the Council’s employer pension 
contributions in 2023/24 and 2024/25, following the triennial actuarial valuation of the 
pension fund. 
Grenfell 
In January 2019, the Council committed £50m of revenue funding to support delivery 
of the Grenfell Recovery Strategy over five years from 2019/20 to 2023/24. In 
December 2020, the Grenfell Recovery Resourcing Framework, building on the 
original strategy, set out plans to work with bereaved individuals, survivors, residents 
and community partners to shape the remaining three years of the programme. 
Unspent funds are carried forward each year, and delivery is now extending beyond 
the current year. 
Insurance 
These funds enable the Council to meet its retained self-insured liabilities 
(deductibles) up to the annual total cap of its financial liabilities. They represent the 
value of future unknown claims, in contrast to the insurance provision which is held 
to manage known liabilities at year-end based on the calculated self-funding value of 
outstanding insurance claims. The Insurance Reserve is also comprised of funds 
held to mitigate against the impact of unforeseen events and emergencies where no 
alternative budget provision exists. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 48 ===
Local Projects 
An amount equal to the annual Council Tax from bereaved individuals and survivors 
of the Grenfell Tower fire will be paid into this reserve each year. Funds are ringfenced, 
focused on providing opportunities for disadvantaged young people and those needing 
support around employment and skills living in social housing in the borough.  
Decisions on how funds are to be spent across projects are taken in accordance with 
the Council’s governance process and subject to Leadership Team approval. 
Proceeds of Crime 
The Proceeds of Crime Act 2002 (POCA) provides for the confiscation or civil 
recovery of the proceeds from crime and contains the principal money laundering 
legislation in the UK. POCA sets out the legislation for the recovery of criminal 
assets, with criminal confiscation being the most used power. The Asset Recovery 
Agency (ARA) was created alongside POCA to monitor financial recovery and use of 
funds. Deemed an incentivisation scheme, any confiscated or collected POCA funds 
were ringfenced and reinvested into crime prevention/reduction projects. 
Recipients of POCA funds, including the Council, were required to complete an 
annual return detailing monies received and information on spend, to demonstrate a 
suitable use of POCA monies. The ARA was closed in 2008 and their powers and 
responsibilities passed to the National Crime Agency (NCA). Although returns have 
not been requested by NCA since pre-pandemic years, in accordance with the terms 
of the incentivisation scheme, a POCA reserve continues. 
Public Health 
Public Health funding supports delivery of the Council Plan, including a recurrent 
investment across the organisation. The grant terms allow the Council to hold a 
balance in its earmarked Public Health Reserve; spending plans are in place and 
have been agreed with the Office for Health Improvements and Disparities (OHID). 
These plans will reduce the reserve balance to around £1.8m by the end of the 
period, and this will be held as a contingency to manage the impact of future 
demand, any unplanned reductions to grant funding, and health protection 
mobilisation.  
Public Health reserves have also been committed to supporting Covid-19 recovery 
and the Council’s response to the cost-of-living crisis. 
Reorganisation 
The Reorganisation Reserve is used to manage transformation-related expenditure, 
and the impact of redundancy costs that cannot be contained within departmental 
budgets. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 49 ===
Street Trading 
The surplus of Street Trading income over expenses is held in a ringfenced reserve 
for investment back into the markets. It is also used to manage the impact of 
unforeseen expenditure in line with the London Local Authorities Act 1990. 
Troubled Families 
This reserve is used to manage fluctuations in service delivery that may otherwise 
occur due to There may be transfers to or from the reserve, according to levels of 
service delivery. 
Schools Reserve 
The Schools Reserve is comprised of cumulative balances that are held on behalf of 
maintained schools across the borough. Based on current trends and known 
pressures, it is anticipated that the reserve will be utilised in full over the next five 
years. However, this position is subject to schools’ funding levels and spend, and 
therefore likely to change. 
Collection Fund 
The Collection Fund is used to manage the surplus or deficit arising from the 
difference between actual and estimated Council Tax and Business Rates collected 
in monetary terms. Balances are subsequently recovered from, or paid back to 
preceptors (The Council, Central Government and the Greater London Authority) by 
forming part of the estimate for the following year.

Funding Public Health 
Grant 
As part of the plans to meet the budget gap, the 
Council is considering redirecting some Public 
Health grant funding from existing activity to 
different cross council programmes directed at 
achieving Public Health outcomes. This would 
enable further General Fund savings. 
A planned total of £1.3m could be saved if the 
Council redirected: 
• £502,000 from core Public Health services; 
and 
• £800,000 from existing cross-Council 
activity currently funded from the core 
Public Health grant, some of which was 
projected to end in March 2026. 
The list below sets out £502k to be released from 
public health services and the associated public 
health risks. Reductions in public health funding 
include a package of efficiencies, reduction and 
ending of some initiatives.

• Removal of Contingency Budget (for
