Appendix 14 CUMULATIVE EQUALITY IMPACT ASSESSMENT OF THE 2026/27 
BUDGET PROPOSALS 
 
1. Executive Summary 
1.1 This paper provides an update on progress on the cumulative equality impact 
assessment for the 2026/27 budget.  
 
1.2 The current cumulative impact of the proposed budget across all protected 
characteristics is assessed as Negative. Some proposals have positive 
impacts and mitigations are being put in place where changes are expected to 
have negative impacts. However, given the scale of the financial challenges 
the Council faces and the subsequent changes to services it is likely there will 
be a major impact on residents. When jointly assessed against the individual 
protected characteristics, the proposals will have a negative impact on age, 
disability, race, pregnancy and maternity and socioeconomic status. Even 
though some of the proposals will have positive impacts, these are not 
currently enough to outweigh the negative impacts of these proposals. 
 
1.3 The impact on all other protected characteristics has been assessed as 
neutral. This is partly due to the lack of data and information. The table in the 
Appendix provides a summary of each proposal and an assessment against 
the protected characteristics that are impacted for each proposal.  
 
1.4 The most significant proposals that have a negative impact on residents 
include:  
• Council Tax Reduction Scheme (CTRS) - The reduction of the CTRS will 
reduce financial support for low-income households, disproportionately 
affecting residents living in our most deprived wards. The mitigations to 
reduce this impact include, strengthening hardship support, ensuring 
discretionary hardship funds are available and accessible for households 
most affected by any reduction in CTRS support, and using detailed caseload 
data to monitor the impact across protected characteristics and adjusting 
mitigation measures as needed. 
• Introducing a self-funder administration fee for those receiving support from 
adult social care who use the Council’s brokerage service may negatively 
impact residents with limited financial resources, particularly older adults and 
those with disabilities who already face high-care costs. The mitigations to 
reduce the negative impact will be to ensure that the price will be competitive 
with the open market, ensuring they will pay less by continuing to use Council 
services.   
• Reductions in digital inclusion initiatives risk increasing digital exclusion 
among residents who cannot afford devices or have insufficient internet 
access. This will disproportionately affect older people, residents with 
disabilities and those from lower socio-economic backgrounds. There are 
currently no mitigations in place should this programme end. Discontinuing 
the service would halt or diminish efforts to support digitally excluded people, 
leading to increased inequality for already disadvantaged populations.  

=== PDF PAGE 258 ===
• Reduction in activities for young people. Although specific services have not 
yet been identified, reductions in youth provision are likely to impact young 
people from socio-economically disadvantaged backgrounds, who depend on 
local services for support and engagement. Mitigations to this service 
reduction are yet to be identified and young people and families must search 
for alternative activities.  
• Reductions in grants offered to VCS organisations will have a negative impact 
on residents due to reductions in services where alternatives cannot be found. 
These organisations support some of the most vulnerable in our communities, 
including those from ethnically diverse communities and those with long term 
health conditions and disabilities. There are limited mitigation options and 
organisations will have to seek alternative funding elsewhere. However, we 
will continue to provide direct funding to VCS through commissioned services 
and grant funding for projects. 
• A reduction in the rent subsidy offered to VCS groups leasing spaces from the 
Council. This proposal again has the potential to negatively impact on 
residents where alternative funding cannot be found. These organisations 
support some of the most vulnerable and diverse communities and once the 
consultation is complete mitigations will be developed as part of the ongoing 
process. 
 
1.5 These proposals will have a disproportionate impact on vulnerable residents 
and deprived communities. Therefore, they will require close monitoring of the 
impact of these decisions should further mitigations need to be introduced to 
support these communities.  
 
 
2. Introduction 
2.1 This paper sets out the equality implications for the Council’s 2026/27 growth 
and savings proposals which have supported the setting of a balanced budget 
for the Council for the next financial year. It outlines the anticipated impact of 
budget proposals on residents who share protected characteristics as defined 
in the Equality Act 2010.  
2.2 Every proposal for service change that results in significant alterations to 
services needs to be accompanied by an Equality Impact Assessment (EqIA). 
The purpose of an EqIA is to ensure that our services meet the needs of our 
local community and to show the likely impact of a proposal on the Council’s 
continuing duty to comply with the Public Sector Equality Duty (PSED). 
2.3 The Council Plan lays out our ambition to becoming the best Council for a 
borough that is greener, safer, and fairer. This commitment includes making 
life fairer for residents in the borough who face the greatest disadvantages 
and challenges and making sure equality, diversity and inclusion are at the 
heart of what we do, so that services are designed to meet the specific needs 
of residents and can be accessed in different ways. Therefore, our 
commitment goes beyond complying with our statutory duties and is integral 
to everything we do as a Council.  

=== PDF PAGE 259 ===
2.4 Furthermore, the Council’s Equality Objective sets out to take targeted action 
to tackle the causes of inequality and deprivation, focusing on areas such as 
health inequalities, employment, and education where the Council can make 
the biggest difference.  
2.5 As the Council’s budgetary pressures become even more challenging in the 
coming years, continuing to understand the equalities considerations of future 
savings proposals will be important as the Council continues to deliver 
savings over the medium term.  
3. Context of Budget Pressure 
3.1 The government is implementing a new funding formula for Local Government 
from 2026/27. It is expected to have an adverse impact of £17.9m in 2026/27. 
3.2 Along with the anticipated impact of funding reform, the Council’s financial 
plans currently budget for inflation to be around 3.8% in 2026/27 (above the 
Bank of England’s 2% target), budget pressures of around £9.7m, and 
additional borrowing costs relating to the Council’s capital programme of £2m. 
Before any savings proposals or decisions on Council Tax a budget gap of 
£36.5m is anticipated for the Council in 2026/27. 
3.3 The sheer scale of the financial challenge and measures needed to meet it to 
deliver a balanced budget presents significant difficulties in maintaining and 
protecting services for residents and service users. In this context, there is 
renewed importance in ensuring that equality implications are considered 
thoroughly, both in relation to individual proposals and in the cumulative 
impact of the Council’s overall financial strategy.  
3.4 As resources become more constrained, it is vital that decisions are made 
with a clear understanding of how they may affect different groups, particularly 
those who may be more vulnerable to changes in service provision. This will 
help ensure the Council continues to meet its statutory duties and upholds its 
commitment to fairness and equality.  
4. Public Sector Equality Duty 
4.1 The purpose of an Equality Impact Assessment is to show the likely 
implications of a proposed decision or activity on groups with protected 
characteristics. The Public Sector Equality Duty places a statutory duty on the 
Council to have due regard to equalities implications in all decisions. 
4.2 The PSED is included in Section 149 of the Equality Act (2010), and places a 
duty on councils to specifically have due regard to: 
- Eliminate discrimination, harassment and victimisation and any other 
conduct prohibited under the Act. 
- Advance equality of opportunity between people who share the protected 
characteristics and those who do not. 
- Foster good relations between people who share the protected 
characteristics and those who do not. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 260 ===
4.3 The Equality Act protects against discrimination based on nine characteristics: 
age, disability, gender reassignment, pregnancy and maternity, race, religion 
or belief, sex, and sexual orientation.  The Council also needs to have due 
regard to the need to eliminate unlawful discrimination against someone 
because of their marriage or civil partnership status. This means that the first 
aim of the duty applies to this characteristic but that the other aims (advancing 
equality and fostering good relations) do not apply. 
4.4 The Council’s approach to equality, diversity, and inclusion (EDI) is central to 
everything we do; whether it is in how we work with our residents, in the services 
we provide or through the recruitment of our workforce. Our commitments are 
outlined in the Council’s Fairer Action Plan 2025 – 2029.  
5. Borough Profile and Local Context   
5.1 Kensington and Chelsea is a diverse and unique central London borough, 
which is rich in culture and heritage. With a population of almost 144,000 
residents, the borough is home to residents of over 50 different nationalities 
who speak over 80 languages.  A summary of data collected from the 2021 
census on protected characteristics as well as the latest joint strategic needs 
assessment is summarised below.  
5.2 Age: The 2021 census shows that the average age of residents in Kensington 
and Chelsea is 40.45 years, making it the fourth oldest population in London, 
with 21.2% of our residents aged 35-49 years and 20.5% of our residents 
aged 50 -64 years. Life expectancy for women in RBKC is the highest in the 
country, for men it is the fifth highest. However, this varies between the north 
and the south of the borough, between people from different ethnic minorities, 
and between homeowners, private renters, and those in social housing.  Life 
expectancy for men in South Kensington is 11 years higher than it is for men 
in Ladbroke Grove. For women in Hans Town, it is 10 years higher than it is 
for women in Ladbroke Grove. 7,800 adults over 65 are estimated to need 
help with at least one self-care activity e.g. getting in and out of bed whilst 1 in 
12 of our older population are living with dementia, and our diagnosis rates 
are below the London average.  
5.3 Disability: Whilst life expectancy is high, residents spend around a fifth of their 
lives in poor health. In the 2021 census, 12.8% of residents in the borough said 
they had a long-term condition or disability that limited their life in some way. In 
the academic year 2024/25, there were 2,688 pupils attending RBKC state 
funded schools with special educational needs, 937 had an education, health 
and care (EHC) plan, and 1,751 were receiving special educational needs 
(SEN) support. 
5.4 Race: Data from the 2021 census shows the population of Kensington and 
Chelsea represents a rich mix of ethnicities. The broad ethnic breakdown of the 
borough’s population is White at 70.6%; Asian, Asian British at 11.8%; Black, 
Black British at 7.9%; Mixed or multiple ethnicities at 6.6%; and Other at 9.9%.  
3 in 10 residents have a long-term condition with those of black ethnic groups 
having almost twice the rate of hypertension.  
5.5 Sex: According to the 2021 census the Kensington and Chelsea population is 
53.2% Female and 46.8% Male. 

=== PDF PAGE 261 ===
5.6 Religion: The 2021 census outlined that Kensington and Chelsea is home to 
people of many beliefs. Around 48% of our residents describe themselves as 
Christian, making it the largest faith group in the borough. This is followed by 
‘No religion’ at 25% and Muslim at 12%. All other religious groups including, 
Buddhist, Hindu, Jewish, Sikh and other religion make up around 5% of the 
population in the borough. Those who identified as Muslim or ‘Other religion’ 
are more likely to say their health is bad or very bad.  
5.7 Gender reassignment: Information on gender reassignment is only captured 
for people aged 16 and above. Of those who answered this question 
approximately 90% of our residents stated that their sex is the same as it was 
at birth. Nearly 9% of residents did not answer the question, but the remaining 
identified themselves as follows; 0.2% said that their sex is different to that of 
their sex registered at birth, 0.1% identify as Trans woman, 0.1% as Trans man, 
less than 0.1% identify as non-binary and then 0.1% identify as other. 
5.8 Marriage/Civil Partnership: Kensington and Chelsea has a high proportion of 
single residents, at 49%. Nearly 35% of residents are married to someone of 
the opposite sex and 0.5% are married to someone of the same sex. Also 
0.15% of our residents are in a civil partnership with someone of the opposite 
sex and 0.39% are in a civil partnership with someone of the same sex. 
5.9 Pregnancy/Maternity: In 2024, there were 1,290 live births in RBK. The total 
fertility rate for RBKC was 1.09 in 2024, below the London (1.35), and England 
(1.41) rates.    
5.10 Sexual Orientation: Information on sexual orientation is only captured 
for people aged 16 and above. The residents in Kensington and Chelsea 
identify themselves in the following way, approximately 85% identify as 
Heterosexual, nearly 3% identify as Gay or Lesbian, 1.3% as Bisexual and 
0.3% as other. 
5.11 Socioeconomic: The 2021 Census highlights certain socio-
demographic factors that are linked to increased risk of specific types of 
deprivation in the borough and indicate areas that we need to pay particular 
attention to. For example, single-parent households are twice as likely to 
experience deprivation in the employment domain, while households paying 
social rent are 4.5 times more likely to face employment-related deprivation. 
Similarly, households where the lead resident cannot speak English well are 
3.5 times more likely to be deprived in the education domain, and those with 
at least one disabled member are 4.5 times more likely to experience 
deprivation overall. Kensington and Chelsea have the second highest rate of 
housing deprivation for people with disabilities across London. In addition, 
Muslim households are 2.2 times more likely to be deprived in the 
employment domain and Black Households are 2.3 times more likely to be 
deprived in the employment domain. The borough has a significant wealth 
disparity among residents; household income in Golborne after housing costs 
is estimated at £22,600. This is almost three times less than the equivalent 
figure for the Brompton and Hans Town area (£63,900).  
  
6. Feedback from the Budget Consultation  
 

=== PDF PAGE 262 ===
6.1 An online public consultation was held between 13th November 2025 
and 9th January 2026. During this period, residents were invited to comment 
on the proposed savings and provide feedback on how the changes might 
affect them. In total, 433 responses were received. We have provided an 
initial analysis of some of the equalities impacts from the consultation below. 
Further work is being undertaken to understand the equalities implications 
across all questions, and this may alter the findings of this EqIA.  
 
 
6.2 A third (33%) of respondents disagreed with the proposed 5% increase in 
Council Tax with affordability and fairness emerging as the most common 
themes in the comments. This feedback reinforces the assessment that 
residents on low incomes, who are disproportionately represented among 
certain protected groups may be adversely affected by the 
proposals, particularly those relating to council tax, digital inclusion, and 
changes to financial support.  
 
6.3 Respondents were asked about savings proposals related to reviewing the 
level and range of services. The proposals included: Reducing funding to the 
Voluntary and Community Sector (separate consultation), opportunities to 
streamline services for young people (saving £100k), stopping delivery of the 
victim advocacy service, redirecting some public health funding and 
acquisitions and development of properties to support service needs in 
temporary accommodation and adult’s and children’s supported living.   
 
6.4 Over half of respondents (54%) supported to some extent, the savings 
proposals put forward to review the level and range of services. 21% opposed 
to some extent, the proposals. The most common theme for opposing these 
proposals was protecting vulnerable and disadvantaged groups, with 
emphasis on safeguarding services for vulnerable populations including 
children, youth, victims, and disadvantaged residents with a focus on funding 
voluntary and community sectors and youth services to prevent future social 
inequalities and costs. Opposition to cuts to public health funding, community 
wellbeing activities, and non-statutory services due to their long-term health 
benefits, cost savings, and importance for community life and cultural events.  
 
6.5 This feedback highlights a clear expectation from residents that vulnerable 
groups should be protected, even in the context of financial pressures. It also 
shows strong recognition of the role the voluntary and community sector plays 
in supporting those at risk and preventing longer-term inequalities. Concerns 
raised about reductions to public health and community wellbeing activities 
suggest that residents understand the wider health impacts and potential 
future costs associated with cutting preventative non-statutory services.  
 
6.6 Respondents were also asked to highlight any ways the proposed budget 
might adversely or unfairly impact people and how these impacts could be 
reduced. The most common theme was the need to protect vulnerable and 

=== PDF PAGE 263 ===
disadvantaged groups, including disabled residents, older adults, low-income 
households, ethnic minority communities, and young people.  
 
6.7 Residents clearly expect the Council to prioritise those who are most at risk of 
being disproportionately affected by budget changes. Their comments 
highlight a strong emphasis on protecting vulnerable and disadvantaged 
groups and ensuring that essential services remain accessible and fair. 
The suggestions for alternative revenue-raising approaches 
also indicates that residents are willing to consider different solutions if it helps 
safeguard vital support for those who need it most. 
 
7. Cumulative Impact of Budget Proposals (2026/27) 
7.1 The table below outlines the impact of the proposals against each protected 
characteristics giving examples of the proposals that have the most significant 
impacts.  
Age Cumulative impact: Negative 
The cumulative impact of the budget proposals on age is assessed as negative. 
Although there are proposals expected to have a positive effect on service support 
for residents across different age groups, particularly older and younger people, 
there are also significant proposals that are assessed as having a negative impact. 
 
The proposals include the following that benefit older residents in RBKC: 
• Additional investment will enable Adult Social Care to manage significant 
financial and operational pressures without reducing services. It ensures the 
Council can continue fulfilling Care Act responsibilities and maintain the 
high standards that recently earned an ‘Outstanding’ rating from the Care 
Quality Commission (CQC). For older residents, this means continued 
access to high-quality, reliable care that promotes independence and 
wellbeing. (98. Adult Social Care: Demographic Pressures) 
• The Lots Road development represents a significant positive impact for 
older residents because it will deliver purpose-built, accessible 
accommodation designed to meet the needs of an ageing population. By 
offering independent living and extra care options, it enables older residents 
to maintain autonomy and wellbeing while accessing tailored support as 
their needs change. The scheme adopts a person-centred approach, 
focusing on outcomes that prevent or delay the need for residential care. (2. 
Adult Social Care: Sheltered Housing: Extra Care) 
• The continuation of prevention initiatives saving will have a positive impact 
on older residents by embedding a strengths-based approach into annual 
reviews. By focusing on individuals’ aspirations and desired outcomes, the 
approach supports older people to live more independently and achieve 
better quality of life. It promotes autonomy and wellbeing while ensuring 
care planning is tailored to personal goals. (4. Adult Social Care: 
Continuation of Prevention Initiatives) 
 
The following proposals are some of those with positive impacts on younger 
residents in RBKC. 

=== PDF PAGE 264 ===
• This proposal involves commissioning a new supported accommodation 
service for 16–17-year-old care leavers, located within 30 minutes of the 
borough. This site is significantly closer than current privately procured 
placements, improving access to local services and helping young people 
build community networks before moving into permanent housing. The 
arrangement will provide 24/7 staffed support in a purpose-built setting, 
ensuring safety and stability. (17. Children’s Services: SIL for 16+). 
• In line with other boroughs across London, RBKC has seen an increasing 
number of children and young people who enter care in adolescence who 
have contextual safeguarding risks. Contextual safeguarding is the 
exploitation and abuse of children, young people and adults where the 
exploitation comes from outside the home. The programme aims to explore 
alternative ways to best meet these young people's care needs. The 
programme aims to make a positive impact on young people by preventing 
care episodes, reducing the time spent in care and ensuring placements 
better meet their needs with clear outcomes and goals identified and 
reviewed. (14. Children’s Services: Contextual Safeguarding).  
• Increasing access to final stage accommodation (social housing) for care 
leavers will benefit young people in care by providing stable 
accommodation in the borough for them to transition to. (13. Children’s 
Services: Children and Housing Alignment) 
 
The following proposals are expected to adversely impact staff but will not 
negatively impact service delivery: 
• 81. Chief Executives: Design Team  
• 12. Children’s Services: Bi-borough Family Services  
• 85. Children’s Services: Creation of an integrated children with 
disabilities service  
• 87. Children’s Services: Virtual School & School Standards  
• 35. Resources and Customer Delivery: Honoraria  
• 36. Resources and Customer Delivery: Starting Salaries  
• 37. Resources and Customer Delivery- HR Travel Card  
• 38. Resources and Customer Delivery - Voluntary Redundancy  
• 72. Resources and Customer Delivery: Council Staff – Internal Flu 
Jabs 
 
 
These proposals have been assessed as having a negative impact on residents: 
• The introduction of a self-funder administration fee will have a negative 
impact on older residents. The Care Act permits councils to charge a fee to 
broker, organise, and monitor care packages. This proposal involves 
developing a fee-based offer for those not eligible for care under the Care 
Act or those self-funding their care who wish to use the Councils brokerage 
service. Given the nature of the service 96% of self-funders are aged 65+. 
This group could be negatively affected by a new charge, but they will still 
pay less for their care overall than if they arranged services on the open 
market. This will be mitigated through significant support that will be 
available to service users, and they are encouraged to approach the 

=== PDF PAGE 265 ===
Council if their financial situation changes. (39. Adult Social Care: Self-
funder Administration Fee) 
• The proposed budget reduction for the Digital Inclusion programme is 
expected to have a significant negative impact on older residents. The 
majority of individuals who access digital inclusion services are older 
residents, and discontinuing the programme will remove a key source of 
assistance, increasing the risk of digital exclusion among this group. (97. 
Resources and Customer Delivery - Digital Inclusion) 
• The Youth proposal involves reviewing statutory and non-statutory services 
for young people across the Council to identify opportunities to streamline 
or reduce provision. The review will cover commissioned services, internal 
teams, and capital investment. Any reduction could negatively impact 
vulnerable and disadvantaged young people by limiting access to activities 
and support in areas of high deprivation, meaning some families may need 
to seek alternative provision. (89. Children’s Services: Youth) 
• The proposal to review the contract for a programme of air quality audits 
within state schools and nurseries in Kensington and Chelsea has been 
assessed as having a negative impact against age. By the end of the 
contract, measures will not yet have been installed to improve air quality in 
schools and nurseries that need it so there will be no improvement for 
young people in state education settings where there is currently poor air 
quality. (59. Environment and Neighbourhoods: Air Quality Project) 
 
 
Disability Cumulative impact: Negative 
The overall cumulative impact of the proposed budget changes is assessed as 
negative. Although mitigations are being put in place where possible, the negative 
impacts are expected to be significant and wide-reaching. There are 12 proposals 
with anticipated positive outcomes, however, these benefits do not fully offset the 
disproportionate impact of the negative proposals. 
 
The below proposals benefit residents with disabilities: 
• Modernising short break facilities for adults with learning disabilities, 
alongside the development of seven supported living units and one staff flat, 
will deliver significant benefits for service users, all of whom have a learning 
disability. (7. Adult Social Care: Specialist accommodation -
Kingsbridge Road and St Helen’s Gardens) 
• Transition to adult services for young people with Special Educational 
Needs and Disabilities (SEND) are often delayed due to unclear legal 
responsibilities and fragmented processes. This saving aims to deliver a 
clear transitions protocol and transparent funding arrangements ensuring 
accountability across services and partners. By strengthening joint planning 
and funding care packages, it will create a smoother, more coordinated 
transition, positively impacting SEND young people as they transition to 
adulthood. (16. Children’s Services: Pathway for Disabilities) 
• The short breaks service is being redesigned to better meet the Care Act 
requirements for adults with learning disabilities with a greater focus on 
planned respite and reduced reliance on unplanned arrangements. The new 
model will provide a local facility, ensuring service users can access support 
close to their networks without needing to travel out of the borough. This 

=== PDF PAGE 266 ===
change will deliver a clear positive impact for adults with learning disabilities 
by improving accessibility to respite services. (78. Adult Social Care: 
Flexible model for short breaks for mental health and learning 
disability service users) 
• The procurement of a pilot concessionary contract to provide additional 
enforcement services will supplement the Street Enforcement Team and is 
expected to reduce the environmental and highways-related offences 
across the borough. These offences obstruct pavements, creating barriers 
for residents with disabilities that may impact their mobility. (89. 
Environment and Neighbourhoods: Street Enforcement - Concession 
Contract)  
• Similarly, the growth bid for cleaner streets aims to enhance waste 
collection and street cleaning standards, making the streets cleaner and 
safer for residents with disabilities that may impact their mobility. (102. 
Environment and Neighbourhoods: Cleanest Streets) 
• 25.3% of residents in temporary accommodation have a long-term disability 
or health condition. The proposal to agree minimum 10-year leases on 
Private Sector Lease (PSL) temporary accommodation will provide greater 
stability and certainty for households awaiting rehousing. This stability will 
positively impact residents with disabilities by reducing the risk of being 
moved from accommodation that meets their needs and preventing 
disruption to established support linked to disability or health. (24. Housing 
and Social Investment: Maximising Housing Income) 
 
These proposals have been assessed as having a negative impact against 
disability. 
 
The following proposals have a negative impact on staff: 
• 81. Chief Executives: Design Team  
• 12. Children’s Services: Bi-borough Family Services  
• 85. Children’s Services: Creation of an integrated children with 
disabilities service  
• 87. Children’s Services: Virtual School & School Standards  
• 35. Resources and Customer Delivery: Honoraria  
• 36. Resources and Customer Delivery: Starting Salaries  
• 37. Resources and Customer Delivery - HR Travel Card  
• 38. Resources and Customer Delivery - Voluntary Redundancy  
• 72. Resources and Customer Delivery: Council Staff – Internal Flu 
Jabs 
 
The following proposals have a negative impact on residents: 
• Two proposals have a negative impact on residents including the 
introduction of a self-funder administration fee. The Care Act permits 
Councils to charge a fee to broker, organise, and monitor care packages. 
This proposal involves developing a fee-based offer for those not eligible for 
care under the Care Act or those self-funding their care who wish to use the 
Councils brokerage service. Given the nature of the service most service 
users are in need of either physical or learning disability support. This group 
could be negatively affected by a new charge, but they will still pay less for 

=== PDF PAGE 267 ===
their care overall than if they arranged services on the open market. 
Significant support will be available to service users, and they are 
encouraged to approach the Council if their financial situation changes. (39. 
Adult Social Care: Self-funder Administration Fee) 
• The second is the review of the Working Age Council Tax Reduction 
Scheme. This proposal involves reviewing the current CTRS for working-
age residents. We have not yet identified the data on the number of 
residents with disabilities who currently receive CTRS and may lose 
eligibility under the revised scheme. However, given the nature of the 
support provided, we anticipate a negative impact on residents with 
disabilities. (76. Resources and Customer Delivery: Review of the 
Working Age Council Tax Reduction Scheme (CTRS)) 
• The proposal to review the contract for air-quality auditing in state nurseries 
and schools in Kensington and Chelsea is likely to have a negative impact 
on young people with disabilities, whose conditions may be worsened by 
poor air quality in education settings. Ending the contract means that 
recommended air quality improvements cannot be implemented, halting the 
ability to address identified risks. (59. Environment and Neighbourhoods: 
Air Quality Project) 
 
Gender Reassignment  Cumulative impact: Neutral 
No proposals identified will have any impact to people with this protected 
characteristic. There has been limited data collected on this characteristic. 
Therefore, based on the data and information available the Council has assumed 
that the savings outlined in this EqIA will have a neutral impact on residents and 
service users from this protected characteristic. We have also highlighted the 
information gap with services, and they have agreed to keep this under review and 
improve data collection on this characteristic in future.  
 
Marriage and Civil Partnership Cumulative impact: Neutral 
No proposals identified will have any impact to people with this protected 
characteristic. There has been limited data collected on this characteristic. 
Therefore, based on the data and information available the Council has assumed 
that the savings outlined in this EqIA will have a neutral impact on residents and 
service users from this protected characteristic. We have also highlighted the 
information gap with services, and they have agreed to keep this under review and 
improve data collection on this characteristic in future.  
 
Pregnancy and Maternity Cumulative impact: Negative 
The cumulative impact of the proposed budget changes on pregnancy and 
maternity is assessed as negative. Most of the anticipated negative impacts relate 
to staff during pregnancy or maternity leave; however, one proposal, the review of 
the Council Tax Reduction Scheme, also has a potential negative impact on 
residents who are pregnant or on maternity leave. While there are some positive 
outcomes for residents, these do not fully offset the risks identified. 
 
The following proposals are expected to have a negative impact on staff: 
• Roles will now be advertised with an expectation that candidates start at the 
first pay spine point, except in exceptional cases. This could disadvantage 

=== PDF PAGE 268 ===
women returning from maternity leave seeking new roles. (36. Resources 
and Customer Delivery: Starting Salaries) 
• There is a potential negative impact against the protected characteristic of 
pregnancy and maternity with voluntary redundancy if measures are not in 
place to ensure employees who are pregnant or on maternity leave do not 
feel pressured or disadvantaged during organisational change, particularly 
in redundancy or voluntary redundancy processes. (38. Resources and 
Customer Delivery - Voluntary Redundancy) 
• The Honoraria proposal seeks to reduce the application of honoraria within 
the organisation and ensure a more robust and consistent approach to their 
use. This change is assessed as having a negative impact on staff during 
pregnancy and maternity. (35. Resources and Customer Delivery: 
Honoraria) 
• This proposal involves removing the Zone 1 and Zone 2 Travel Card, which 
currently forms part of an employee benefit and is claimed based on the 
number of days an employee attends the office. While further analysis is 
required to understand the full impact on pregnant employees using current 
travel card data, this change is assessed as having a negative impact on 
staff during pregnancy and maternity. (37. Resources and Customer 
Delivery - HR Travel Card) 
• The Council Tax Reduction Scheme (CTRS) proposal seeks to reduce the 
CTRS offer to residents meaning some households that currently pay no 
council tax will be required to contribute a proportion of the charge. While 
specific data on households with pregnant individuals or those who have 
recently given birth is not yet available, the expected impact on residents 
under the pregnancy and maternity protected characteristic is assessed as 
negative. Further research will be undertaken to better understand the scale 
of the impact. (76. Resources and Customer Delivery: Review of the 
Working Age Council Tax Reduction Scheme (CTRS)) 
• The proposal to cease the provision of the free internal flu vaccination 
service for RBKC staff is expected to have a negative impact on staff who 
are pregnant or on maternity leave. While planned mitigations include a 
communications programme to ensure pregnant staff are aware that they 
are eligible for a free flu jab through the NHS, this does not support those 
who are unaware of their pregnancy at the time of vaccination or those who 
have recently given birth and are on maternity leave post-partum. The 
current internal offer also enables pregnant staff to receive a vaccination 
conveniently at their workplace, avoiding the need to travel. Removing this 
option may create an additional barrier for some staff, who will now need to 
attend external NHS providers to access their free jab. (72. Resources and 
Customer Delivery: Council Staff – Internal Flu Jabs) 
 
Despite these challenges there are proposals that will deliver benefits for residents 
during pregnancy and maternity.  
• The proposal to secure longer leases on temporary accommodation is 
assessed as having a positive impact on residents under the pregnancy and 
maternity protected characteristic, ensuring families have access to stable 
accommodation. (24. Housing and Social Investment: Maximising 
housing income) 

=== PDF PAGE 269 ===
• The investment in temporary accommodation from the RBKC pension fund 
will increase the supply and quality of temporary accommodation, reducing 
the reliance on short-term housing arrangements improving stability of 
housing for residents who are pregnant or have young children. (25. 
Housing and Social Investment: Investment in TA from pension fund) 
 
Greater stability in temporary accommodation will benefit mothers who have 
recently given birth by lowering the risk of being moved from properties that meet 
their needs and preventing disruption to established support linked to maternity.  
 
Race Cumulative impact: Negative 
The cumulative impact of the proposed budget changes on race is assessed as 
negative. While there are some anticipated positive outcomes, particularly through 
growth proposals that respond to demographic pressures, these do not outweigh 
the negative impacts identified, most of which impact staff.  
 
The following growth proposals are expected to have a positive impact on race by 
addressing demographic pressures and improving service delivery for diverse 
communities.  
• Additional investment will help meet the needs of an increasingly diverse 
population requiring adult social care services. A higher proportion of 
racially diverse residents access adult social care compared to their overall 
representation in the borough, meaning the growth in funding allowing 
services to continue will directly benefit these communities. (98. Adult 
Social Care: Demographic Pressures) 
• The Social Services Customer Service Team is supporting vulnerable 
residents by managing all incoming contacts for Adults’ and Children’s 
Social Services. Rising demand threatens service quality and safeguarding, 
requiring additional resources to maintain efficiency and protect residents. 
This proposal will have a positive impact on race as over half of the 
borough’s looked after children are from black or mixed ethnic backgrounds 
meaning investment will directly benefit racially diverse communities who 
rely on these services. (100. Children’s Services: Customer Delivery 
Social Services Line) 
• Growth funding will support services for children and families. As with adult 
social care, children’s services see an overrepresentation of racially diverse 
families accessing support compared to their proportion in the borough’s 
population, so this investment will help ensure equitable access and 
improved outcomes. (101. Children’s Services: Demographic Pressures) 
 
Most proposals assessed as having a negative impact on race relate to staff such 
as: 
• 81. Chief Executives: Design Team  
• 12. Children’s Services: Bi-borough Family Services  
• 85. Children’s Services: Creation of an integrated children with 
disabilities service  
• 87. Children’s Services: Virtual School & School Standards  
• 35. Resources and Customer Delivery: Honoraria 
• 36. Resources and Customer Delivery: Starting Salaries  

=== PDF PAGE 270 ===
• 37. Resources and Customer Delivery: HR Travel Card  
• 38. Resources and Customer Delivery: Voluntary Redundancy  
 
These proposals have a negative impact on residents: 
• The review of the Working Age Council Tax Reduction Scheme will have a 
negative impact against the protected characteristic race; whilst the specific 
data on the racial background of residents currently benefitting from the 
scheme is not available, it is anticipated that changes could 
disproportionately affect racially diverse households. Further analysis will be 
undertaken to better understand the scale of this impact. (76. Resources 
and Customer Delivery: Review of the Working Age Council Tax 
Reduction Scheme (CTRS)) 
• The Youth services proposal seeks to identify opportunities to streamline or 
reduce the offer of youth services across the borough. The specific data on 
the race of young people who may be impacted is not currently available 
the impact has been assessed as negative. Funding for voluntary sector 
organisations working with young people is generally focused on supporting 
vulnerable and disadvantaged children, young people and their families in 
areas of high deprivation within the borough where there is a high up take 
from residents from global majority backgrounds. (89. Children’s Services: 
Youth)

assessed as neutral. While there are some anticipated positive impacts,

Social Care services, most users are either Christian or Muslim, with Muslim

a positive impact on religion and belief: 
• Through annual reviews and strengths-based approach, the continuation of 
prevention initiatives will support service users to live more independently 
and achieve better outcomes, which will benefit communities who are over-
represented in adult social care. (4. Adult Social Care: Continuation of 
Prevention Initiatives) 
• Enhancing integration with Housing will make more appropriate and flexible 
housing available at the point of discharge, creating options for people with 
learning and physical disabilities and mental health needs, including diverse 
service users. (79. Adult Social Care: Increased nominations to general 
needs housing) 
• Capital works will modernise the existing in-borough offer and create more 
accommodation options for service users who want to live in RBKC but are 
currently placed outside the borough. (7. Adult Social Care: Specialist 
accommodation -Kingsbridge Road and St Helen’s Garden) 
• Additional resources will help meet demographic pressures and ensure 
services remain responsive to the needs of diverse communities, improving 

=== PDF PAGE 271 ===
access and outcomes for residents. (98. Adult Social Care: Demographic 
Pressures) 
 
Most proposals with a negative impact staff rather than residents including: 
• 35. Resources and Customer Delivery: Honoraria  
• 36. Resources and Customer Delivery: Starting Salaries 
• 37. Resources and Customer Delivery - HR Travel Card  
• 38. Resources and Customer Delivery - Voluntary Redundancy  
 
Sex Cumulative impact: Neutral

The proportion of adult social care service users who are female (64%) is 
significantly higher than the proportion of women in the borough’s population 
(53.2%, Census). As a result, the introduction of the self-funder administration fee 
is likely to have a greater negative impact on women than men, given their 
overrepresentation among service users. (39. Adult Social Care: Self-funder 
Administration Fee) 
 
The following Children’s Services proposals have an assessed positive impact in 
relation to sex. Currently, 71% of our looked after children are male, and these 
proposals are expected to benefit this group by addressing their specific needs 
and improving outcomes. 
• Increasing the number of final stage offers made annually to reduce the 
disparity between demand and supply of final stage accommodation will 
reduce the wait time for care leavers who are ready to live independently 
and in turn, reduce accommodation costs to the Council. (13. Children’s 
Services: Children and Housing Alignment) 
• The newly identified site for accommodating 16 and 17 year olds is within 
30 minutes of the borough and is closer to the borough than where they are 
living in privately commissioned semi-independent living (SIL). (17. 
Children’s Services: SIL for 16+) 
• This proposal will ensure continued access to the customer service line, 
which is integral for residents who need to contact Children’s Services. 
Maintaining this provision is essential to ensuring families can seek support. 
(100. Children’s Services: Customer Delivery Social Services Line) 

=== PDF PAGE 272 ===
• Growth funding will support services for children and families. A higher 
proportion of young male looked after children access support than the 
borough average, so this investment will help ensure equitable access and 
improved outcomes. (101. Children’s Services: Demographic Pressures) 
 
Sexual Orientation Cumulative impact: Neutral 
No proposals identified will have any impact to people with this protected 
characteristic. There is limited data collected on this characteristic by the services 
outlining budget proposals. Therefore, based on the data and information available 
the Council has assumed that the savings outlined in this EqIA will have a neutral 
impact on residents and service users from this protected characteristic. We have 
also highlighted the information gap with services, and they have agreed to keep 
this under review and improve data collection on this characteristic in future.  
Socio-economic and Geographical Cumulative impact: Negative 
The overall cumulative impact of the proposed budget changes in relation to both 
socioeconomic status and geographical position is assessed as negative. While 
some proposals may deliver positive outcomes, particularly in relation to 
socioeconomic status, a significant proportion of impacts are negative, impacting 
both staff and residents.  
 
Proposals with a negative impact on residents against the protected characteristics 
socioeconomic and geographical position include: 
• Reducing investment in digital inclusion initiatives may disproportionately 
affect residents on lower incomes who lack access to devices or reliable 
internet. It may also impact those who rely on local drop-in sessions 
delivered across the borough, creating barriers to accessing online services 
and information, increasing the risk of digital exclusion. (97. Resources 
and Customer Delivery - Digital Inclusion) 
• The Council is looking to drive down the number of printed assets as part of 
its design and communications approach. While this supports efficiency and 
sustainability, it may negatively impact residents who are digitally excluded 
and rely on printed materials to access important information from the 
Council. This could disproportionately impact individuals with limited access 
to digital devices or internet connectivity, as well as those who rely on 
printed information from the Council that is distributed through community 
hubs and local spaces. (81. Chief Executives: Design Team) 
• Reductions in youth services across the borough may negatively impact 
young people from disadvantaged backgrounds who rely on these services 
and targeted interventions. Reduced provision may limit access for those 
unable to travel to alternative locations, particularly in areas with higher 
levels of deprivation. (89. Children’s Services: Youth) 
• The review of the Working Age CTRS will result in reduced eligibility or 
support for low-income households. This change is likely to have a direct 
negative impact on residents experiencing financial hardship as well as 
residents living in more deprived areas of the borough, increasing financial 
inequality across wards. (76. Resources and Customer Delivery: Review 
of the Working Age Council Tax Reduction Scheme (CTRS)) 
• Introducing a fee for self-funders who use the Council’s brokerage service 
may negatively impact residents with limited financial resources who are not 

=== PDF PAGE 273 ===
eligible for Care Act support. While the fee is lower than market rates, it 
introduces an additional cost for individuals already managing care 
expenses, which could disproportionately affect residents on lower incomes. 
(39. Adult Social Care: Self-funder Administration Fee) 
• With the information currently available, the results of the air-quality audits 
and the associated recommendations for individual schools are not known. 
However, halting the contract is likely to have a disproportionate impact 
against socio-economic and geographical, as schools will no longer receive 
the recommended measures needed to improve air quality. Schools serving 
children from parts of the borough that already experience poor air quality 
will be particularly disadvantaged, as pupils may continue to be exposed to 
poor air quality without any mitigation. (59. Environment and 
Neighbourhoods: Air Quality Project) 
 
 
8. Conclusion and recommendations 
 
8.1 This report has concluded that the cumulative impact of budget savings for 
2026/27 will have a negative impact on residents of the borough and staff.  
 
8.2 Services will be expected to monitor the anticipated outcomes of these 
proposals as the initiatives and services develop. This will include updating 
the EqIA and regular equalities monitoring and analysis to evaluate trends and 
identify actions as part of implementing the budget. Lead members and senior 
managers will have responsibility for overseeing this as decisions are made 
and service changes take place. 
 
8.3 The following approach is therefore recommended: 
• Given the significant financial challenges and the potential for negative 
impacts services should closely monitor the EqIA. It should be reviewed 
regularly and updated it to ensure any negative impacts are mitigated or 
services changes are made to reduce the negative impact.  
• Early engagement with EqIA completion – services should clearly identify and 
consider the specific needs of different groups at an early stage of 
implementing their proposals to support effective decision making. 
• Data gaps – where services have identified data gaps in the EqIA process, 
steps should be taken to address these in the forthcoming year. This will 
enable better modelling of potential impacts and assessments in the future. 
• Engagement – the Council should continue engagement with all residents and 
service users to identify ways in which services can be improved and meet 
diverse needs and are culturally appropriate.  
• Review EqIAs as activities are delivered and savings are realised and 
implement necessary changes as identified through equalities monitoring. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 274 ===
Appendix 
The table below outlines: 
• Each proposal under its saving theme.  
• The initial equalities assessment (Positive, Negative, Neutral) for each 
proposal by protected characteristic provided by the service area.  
• A high-level summary of the rationale for this assessment by the service area.  
• Where further activity is required to better understand the equalities 
implications of a proposal.  
Proposals have been grouped under four main themes that summarise the ambition 
of the proposals. The first is, Better Ways of Working, these savings relate to 
initiatives that aim to increase development of specialised housing options for a 
range of residents, support residents to live more independently, better working 
practices across departments to support residents more holistically and tighter 
workforce controls and reductions of some staff benefits. 
 
The second theme is to use these proposals to Be More Businesslike. These 
mostly relate to opportunities to raise income via advertising, museums and culture 
income opportunities, whilst ensuring they do not become inaccessible to low-
income residents, or increases in fees such as parking, in line with London market 
rates.  
 
The third theme is achieving Service Change, outlining proposals for how services 
can and will be delivered differently to deliver savings.  
 
The final theme focuses on proposals intended to Generate Extra Income. This will 
be achieved by reducing funding for selected services, implementing new charges to 
generate revenue, or pursuing alternative funding sources, whilst some proposals 
include direct reductions to grants.

77. Children’s Services: 
Transfer responsibility to 
local primary schools 
(and reduce overheads) 
for delivering affordable 
childcare at two in-
house north Kensington 
day nurseries 
Saving Unknown 
The proposal will have an 
impact on 23 staff who will 
transfer under school 
management as part of this 
proposal. It will be necessary to 
conduct a full EQIA as part of 
the transfer process. It is not 
possible to complete this until 
the transfer dates are finalised 
with the school. At the point of 
transfer, there will be no staff 
redundancies. If, during the 
transitions period, 
redundancies are required, this 
will be the responsibility of the 
school who will need to 
conduct their own consultation 
and complete appropriate 
EQIAs as part of the formal 
staff consultation process. 
 
The transfer of the nurseries to 
the schools will have no direct 
impact on the nursery offer at 
the point of transfer and 
schools are required to 
maintain fee levels over a three 
year period post transfer. 
Beyond this date, there may be 
changes to the offer and 
charges. This will be the 
responsibility of schools to lead 
the consultation and complete 
appropriate EQIAs. It is our 
expectation that we will work in 
partnership with schools during 
this process. Recent 
experiences with similar 
transfers in other nurseries 
have been positive with no 
drop in quality or offer. Our 
expectation is that this offer 
would be the same.  
 
EqIA to be 
produced 
and 
reviewed 
once 
project has 
progressed.

required at 
this stage.  
103. Resources and 
Customer Delivery: 
Cyber Security 
Growth No impact on equalities 
The proposed programme will 
affect all residents, service 
users, clients, staff or other 
data subjects in the same way: 
the personal data held on them 
will be better protected, 
including from unauthorised 
access and from cyber attacks, 
or any other security event 
which can lead to an inability of 
the council to deliver statutory 
services. 
No further 
action 
required at 
this stage.  
104. Resources and 
Customer Delivery: 
Cyber Security Zero 
Trust Architecture 
Growth No impact on equalities 
The proposal will improve 
service resilience in the event 
of a cyber event or attack. The 
proposed programme will 
impact all residents, service 
users, or other data subjects in 
the same way improving the 
protection of their data. 
Meaning no disproportionate 
impact to any protected 
characteristics and no 
equalities implications. 
No further 
action 
required at 
this stage.  
105. Resources and 
Customer Delivery: 
Oracle 
 
Growth 
No impact on 
equalities  
This proposal is not expected 
to have equalities implications, 
as it maintains the existing 
Oracle HR service and fulfils a 
contractual commitment. It 
does not affect service access, 
delivery, or staff experience in 
a way that would impact 
protected groups. 
No further 
action 
required at 
this stage. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 298 ===
 
1 
 
 
Appendix 15 
 
 
Schedule of Draft Fees and Charges 2026/27 
 
 
This document includes fees and charges that have been proposed for 202 6/27 and 
in some cases 2027/28 for services which need to facilitate advance bookings. 
The proposed fees and charges are presented by directorate for the purposes of this 
report.  
 
There are increases that broadly follow the 5% increase set out in the covering budget 
report with some small deviation either in percentage or absolute terms. For example, 
where the fee or charge is only a few pence, a small change appears as a larger % 
increase.

i.e. if a charge is applied by the customer’s bank for making a payment, e.g. in the

Explanatory Notes  
Clarification on the cost of traffic orders  
Traffic order changes that have been requested for commercial purposes will be 
charged for. 
For permanent traffic order changes included in the four monthly Miscellaneous 
Parking Amendment Order: for items to count as one change the following conditions 
will need to be met:  
• items should be linked, e.g. if a pay-by-phone visitor parking bay is converted 
to a resident bay to make up for the conversion of a resident space to another 
use; and  
• items should fit on the same street notice when the map is at the scale of 
1:625.  
Occasionally more than one change will fit on the same street notice. In such cases, 
each set of linked items will be charged as one change.  
Where a scheme involves conversion of a number of bays for the same reason, e.g. 
a new car club operator joins the market, each bay will be charged for as a separate 
change.

The cost of parking suspensions is charged separately to the cost of making a 
temporary or emergency temporary traffic regulation order or the cost of closing a 
road for a relevant (special) event.  
The cost of suspensions will apply on every day that public access to the site is 
physically possible, i.e. the road is not securely fenced off.

SERVICE: PARKING  
 
All charges provided below relate to the amount of money that the Council receives, 
i.e. if a charge is applied by the customer’s bank for making a payment, e.g. in the 
case of cheques or transfers from foreign banks (where accepted), then this charge 
is payable by the customer in addition to the charges below. It should be noted that: 
 
• if the charges are not paid in full, the application will not proceed until the full 
amount is received by the Council; and 
• any false claims will not be refunded.  
 
In previous years, the cost of parking permits, visitor parking, suspensions and 
dispensations were increased in line with the September Retail Price Index (RPI), 
which this year was 4.5 per cent. Parking permit increases are proposed at 5% in 
line with RPI (rounded).   
 
The Council sets parking charges in order to balance the needs of local residents, 
visitors and local business, whilst ensuring that charges reflect the cost of investing 
in and delivering essential services for our community. Visitor parking charges are 
proposed to increase by 10%.   
 
 Charges for parking suspensions and dispensations are linked to visitor parking 
charges and so are also proposed to increase by 10%. 
 
Prices are rounded to the nearest £1 and therefore in some cases the percentage 
increase is higher or lower than the inflationary figure given above. The percentage 

=== PDF PAGE 338 ===
 
41 
 
increase in the cost of a resident’s parking permit will vary according to the vehicle’s 
CO2 emissions (see Explanatory Notes).

Explanatory notes: 
Resident permit charges 
The charge for residents’ parking permits has two elements: 
• base permit price 
• surcharge per gramme of CO2 per km 
 
When we changed to the emissions-based resident parking permit structure, the base 
permit price was the same for all permit duration periods, i.e. 3 month, 6 month and 
12 month and reflected the cost of issuing a permit.  
From 1 April 2025, the base permit price was £63 and the annual cost per gramme 
of CO2 per km was £1. The average cost of an annual residents’ permit, including 

=== PDF PAGE 343 ===
 
46 
 
the base price, was £205. A five per cent inflationary increase in the average cost of 
a residents’ permit is therefore £10.  
 
 
Diesel surcharge 
In April 2021, we changed the resident parking permit structure to provide a greater 
incentive for residents to choose a cleaner vehicle. Further details can be found in 
the Key Decision Report link below. 
https://www.rbkc.gov.uk/howwegovern/keydecisions/decision.aspx?DecisionID=5520 
One of the recommendations of the report was that the diesel surcharge be 
increased at a rate above the general rate of inflation from 2022/23 onwards to 
encourage residents to choose petrol or electric vehicles. Therefore the 12-month 
diesel surcharge has been increased by more than other fuel sources and rounded 
to the nearest pound. The six month and three-month diesel surcharge price are a 
half and a quarter of the annual cost respectively. 
 
 
Visitor motorcycle parking tariff 
Although we charge residents to park motorcycles in standard residents' bays, we do 
not charge residents to park in resident permit motorcycle bays.  
When proof of payment for visitor parking required a Pay-and-Display ticket, 
motorcycles were made exempt due to their not having a secure mechanism for 
displaying such tickets. That limitation does not apply now that payment is made by 
Pay-by-phone so we proposed to charge in April 2023. Charging for visitor 
motorcycle parking will reduce the incidence of motorcycles being left in bays for 
long periods of time, thereby releasing more space for daily visitors. We have not yet 
implemented this charge for visitor motorcycle parking. 
Electric motorcycles will continue to park for free in visitor motorcycle bays, as will 
motorcycles with a valid residents' motorcycle permit. 
All visitor motorcycle bays have the same Pay-by-phone location code and so 
motorcyclists are able to park in multiple visitor motorcycle bays on the same day 
without having to pay again. 
From 1 April 2023, all our motorcycle bays became shared use bays (subject to the 
usual consideration of traffic order objections) so can be used by visitors (who pay) 
and residents (who can park for free with a permit). This provides a total of 312 bays 
available to motorcyclists. 
Visitor parking tariffs  
We link visitor parking tariffs to our inflation measure to ensure that they keep pace 
with inflation. In this way, parking tariffs discourage non-essential trips and increase 

=== PDF PAGE 344 ===
 
47 
 
the opportunity for motorists to find a vacant space without excessive searching. We 
review charges each year, taking account of the change in RPI since the previous 
increase, but it does not necessarily mean we increase them every year.  
We last increased visitor parking tariffs, across all tariff bands in April 2025, following 
the change introduced in April 2023 when the base price for visitor parking became 
the electric vehicle tariff, to make it easier to maintain a differential of circa £1 per 
hour between the four tariff areas. The petrol tariff is now set to be 33 per cent more 
than the electric vehicle tariff (rounded to the nearest 10p) and the diesel tariff is now 
set to be 50 per cent more than the electric vehicle tariff (rounded to the nearest 
10p).   
 
The actual charges are based on applying the four per cent inflationary increase to 
the  electric vehicle price for each of the four tariff areas.

Suspension and dispensation charges  
Suspension and dispensation charges are based on the tariff in the high tariff area for 
petrol vehicles and are calculated as follows:  
i) Fewer than six chargeable days (per space per chargeable day) – this is 
11 times the cost of the hourly petrol tariff in the high tariff area, rounded to 
the nearest pound;  
ii) Six to 42 chargeable days inclusive (per space per chargeable day) – this 
is 1.5 times the cost of the multiple of the petrol tariff outlined in (i);  
iii) 43 chargeable days or more (per space per chargeable day) – this is twice 
the cost of the multiple of the petrol tariff outlined in (i).  
As the cost of the petrol tariff in the high tariff area has increased this year, so too 
has the cost of suspensions and dispensations.  
  

=== PDF PAGE 345 ===
 
48 
 
Express suspension charge 
On 1 April 2023, we introduced an express suspension charge which requires five 
working days’ notice rather than the standard ten working days’ notice. This is a one-
off cost per application and was priced at five times the “fewer than six chargeable 
days” charge in that year. From 1 April 2026, this cost will be increased by 10% 
(rounded where appropriate). 
 
Clarification of the cost of suspensions  
 
It has come to officers’ attention that some customers are swapping the applicant 
names on suspension bookings so that they avoid the higher charges for longer 
suspensions. For clarification, we apply the higher charge for suspensions when the 
cumulative days relate to the same planning permission rather than by applicant for 
suspensions which have an associated planning permission. We also reserve the 
right to apply the higher charge where it has evidence of swapping between 
applicants, or other behaviour, to avoid the higher charge. Suspensions for bays 
behind hoardings will be charged for every day that the bay is unusable.  
 
Administrative charges  
 
We do not levy an administration charge for residents transferring a private number 
plate to a different vehicle. 
 
The administrative charge for antique dealer permits was last increased in April 
2025. From 1 April 2026, this charge will rise by 10%.  
 
  

=== PDF PAGE 346 ===
 
49 
 
DIRECTORATE: ADULT SOCIAL CARE & PUBLIC HEALTH 
DEPARTMENT: ADULT SOCIAL CARE  
 
SERVICE: ADULT SOCIAL CARE  
 
Councils are required to have a client contribution policy that is demonstrably fair 
and does not undermine the overall objectives of social care – to promote both 
independence and social inclusion of individuals. A Contributions Policy is in place. A 
financial assessment is undertaken in line with the Contributions Policy and 
determines for each individual, the financial contribution that they are asked to make 
towards the cost of their care. The amount an individual contributes must be an 
‘affordable’ amount, based on their ability to pay. The table below sets out the fees 
and charges for Adult Social Care services. Within Adult Social Care there are some 
charges for which the charging framework is set nationally, such as residential and 
nursing care. These are excluded from the fees and charges set out below.
